Member states not sharing info creates security gaps – auditors
2026-09-22

Member states not sharing info creates security gaps – auditors

Silicon Republic | 2026-09-22

Visit Original Website
Ireland was among the countries evaluated by the European Court of Auditors. A new report published by the European Court of Auditors (ECA) has found that information sharing, or rather the lack thereof, among EU member states is creating gaps in cybersecurity for the region. Among the countries investigated for the purpose of the ECA’s research were Ireland, Greece and Italy. The report found that cybersecurity incidents have the potential to disrupt public services, businesses, critical infrastructure and the EU’s internal market. Largely, the responsibility for responding to cyber incidents is placed on member states, but the wider EU community plays a critical role. The ECA, in a statement said, “This is the case particularly when such incidents cause major disruption, significant financial losses, or substantial harm to people or organisations (significant cybersecurity incidents), or when they affect several member states and go beyond the capacity of a single country to respond effectively (large-scale cybersecurity incidents).” According to the report, the EU has been increasingly investing in stronger cybersecurity via the 2021-2027 EU budget and the Digital Europe Programme, which has provided funding of €1.4bn and is the main source of the bloc’s cybersecurity funding. Despite this, however, the ECA auditors are of the opinion that there is a clear ‘Achilles heel’ element, in that Europe’s cybersecurity system is too dependent on exchanges of information that are proving to be insufficient. “The Cyber Blueprint, which was adopted in 2025, largely clarifies roles and responsibilities by setting out how the EU should manage major cybersecurity crises. However, the way the two EU cyber networks work together has still not been formally defined,” said the ECA. “This hampers the cooperation of the ‘CSIRTs’ network, which brings together national teams dealing with cyber incidents, and ‘EU-Cyclone’, an EU network for cyber crisis cooperation.” Additionally, some EU countries are still in the process of updating their cybersecurity policies in a landscape where national security rules are limiting the level of information that can be shared. The ECA claims this is resulting in an environment in which EU networks may struggle to detect threats early on, or coordinate an effective response. This is further exacerbated, it said, by an overlap in EU bodies responsible for monitoring cybersecurity threats. Commenting on the findings, George-Marius Hyzler, the ECA member in charge of the audit, said, “The EU has made progress in building a cybersecurity cooperation framework, but it is not yet working as effectively as it should. “When a serious cyber incident occurs, timely and actionable information is essential. Without it, networks and mechanisms lose much of their added value.” Don’t miss out on the knowledge you need to succeed. EU proposes common energy rating scheme for data centres
2026-09-22

EU proposes common energy rating scheme for data centres

Silicon Republic | 2026-09-22

Visit Original Website
The bloc is seeking to mitigate the impact of the energy consumption necessary to its goal of tripling data centre capacity over the next five to seven years. The EU wants to implement a common energy rating scheme for data centres with a capacity of more than 500kW, aimed at increasing transparency around their energy use and supporting their sustainable integration into Europe’s energy system. The bloc is seeking to mitigate the impact of the energy consumption necessary to its goal of tripling data centre capacity over the next five to seven years as part of its push for digital and AI sovereignty and self-determination. The ongoing global AI race has caused a surge in the deployment of data centres and associated power consumption. According to the EU, data centres in the bloc are on track to consume 114TWh annually by 2030, amounting to 3pc of its overall electricity demand. In 2024, they accounted for 68TWh. “In addition to using more energy, an increase of data centres could put extra pressure on electricity grids and the use of natural resources, such as water, and contribute to increased carbon emissions,” the European Commission said yesterday (21 September). “To limit these impacts, it is essential that data centres are highly energy- and water-efficient and sustainably integrated in the energy system.” The scheme would will also cover data centres’ contribution to power grids through methods such as the reuse of waste heat or the addition of clean energy generation capacities. “Sustainable, flexible and well-designed data centres – that can adjust their electricity use to the grid’s conditions – can help lower the overall cost of the electricity system, improve grid stability and integrate more renewable energy,” the Commission said. The Commission has launched a call for evidence and public consultation on minimum performance standards for data centres operating in Europe, which will run until 14 December. “Tripling our data centre capacity cannot mean tripling the pressure on our grids, our water and our energy bills. That starts with transparency, rating large data centres depending on their impact on our energy system,” said Teresa Ribera, the Commission’s executive vice-president for clean, just and competitive transition. “Efficient, flexible, powered by additional clean energy and reusing waste heat – this is how data centres become an asset for the EU’s energy transition.” The legal proposal establishing the common rating scheme is now subject to a two-month scrutiny period with the European Parliament and the European Council, which can object to it, but not alter it before it enters into force. “To ensure that the digital transformation truly works for our society, we must address the growing energy demand of data centres. Understanding their energy and resource consumption is the essential first step towards integrating them sustainably into our energy system. Digital sovereignty must go hand-in-hand with energy responsibility,” said Dan Jørgensen, the EU’s commissioner for energy and housing. The first sustainability labels for individual data centres are expected to be displayed in 2027, according to the Commission. Don’t miss out on the knowledge you need to succeed. Netherlands’ Morphotonics bags €40m to scale AI glasses parts manufacture
2026-09-22

Netherlands’ Morphotonics bags €40m to scale AI glasses parts manufacture

Silicon Republic | 2026-09-22

Visit Original Website
3M Ventures, the European Innovation Council and European Investment Bank were among its Series B backers. Dutch deep-tech company Morphotonics has raised more than €40m in a Series B round for scaling its technology that helps build AI glasses. Founded in 2014, Morphotonics develops platforms for large-area nanoimprint lithography, a high-resolution fabrication technique used to create miniscule surface patterns by physically replicating the intricacies of a precision stamp into a material. Unlike conventional photolithography, which uses light, nanoimprinting mechanically replicates patterns, allowing for accurate and repeatable fabrication with better precision. The process allows for better replication on large areas, according to the company, and is used to manufacture advanced optical components such as augmented reality waveguides – essential for AI glasses – and semiconductor structures. AI glasses have sharply risen in popularity in the wearable devices category, driven largely by Meta’s growing catalogue of products. In H2 last year, AI glasses alone accounted for 88pc of the total shipment of smart glasses, while Morphotonics cited expectations placing consumer spend for smart glasses at $44bn between now and 2032. The Dutch company’s expanded Series B was supported by 3M Ventures, Innovation Industries and BOM, alongside Invest-NL, the European Innovation Council, Ernij Next and the European Investment Bank (EIB). Over the past year, Morphotonics has been developing its Cypris X700 platform, specifically designed to produce AR waveguides at high volumes. The new funding will be used to increase its production capacity, as well as advance product development and expand teams, the company said. Morphotonics, headquartered in Veldhoven, opened an office in China last year to better collaborate with its Asian customers. Revenue at the company tripled last year, compared to 2024, and it expects “substantial” further growth. “Everyone is talking about AI glasses right now. AI is moving off the screen and into the real world, into glasses, spatial systems and a new generation of intelligent devices,” said Hugo da Silva, the CEO of Morphotonics. “But these and similar devices will not reach mass adoption unless the optical components inside them can be manufactured at consumer-electronics scale. “Our role is to make that possible. By bringing nanoimprint lithography from specialist production into high-volume manufacturing, we’ll ensure the AI glasses and physical AI future can arrive. We can help reduce one of the key manufacturing barriers facing AI glasses and other advanced optical devices.” EIB Group office head for the Netherlands Chantal Schrijver said: “If Europe wants to stay competitive, it needs to stay in front when it comes to cutting-edge technology. “One of our main challenges is to make sure that local businesses can stay and grow in Europe, and the public and private sectors need to make sure that these companies can get the right type of financing to do so.” Don’t miss out on the knowledge you need to succeed. Wild New Website Lets You Shoot “Less Lethal” Ammunition From Gun Turrets in a Firing Range
2026-09-22

Wild New Website Lets You Shoot “Less Lethal” Ammunition From Gun Turrets in a Firing Range

Futurism | 2026-09-22

Visit Original Website
I’ve never shot a gun before — and it’s not an activity I’d actively seek out, given the grim implications of trivializing an act of violence by transforming it into recreation. Yet it’s never been easier to fire a weapon. Now, in fact, it doesn’t even require you to get off your couch. A just-launched website called Shov allows users to “shoot guns on the internet” by remotely-controlling a weapon right inside their web browser. The pneumatic weapons fire .68 caliber hardened rubber “less lethal” munitions, which have killed dozens and wounded thousands worldwide when they’ve been deployed by police. The is provocative, launching as remotely-operated weapons becomes a common sight on real-world battlefields. Back in 2021, Israel used a similar remote-control gun to assassinate a top Iranian nuclear scientist. In Ukraine, militaries on both sides have used explosives-laden drones as weapons, and even reportedly taken the concept to its natural conclusion by allowing AI-powered autonomous weapons to identify and strike targets without human intervention. While Shov certainly isn’t recreating the intricate horrors of modern warfare, the very proposition — allowing anybody to assume control over a “gun” turret that could easily be outfitted with a deadly assault rifle — feels laden with dark baggage. A live feed show a long metal structure with a dozen .68-caliber pneumatic guns mounted to it, each of which appear to be aiming and firing independently. With the sound turned on, it’s an ear-splitting chorus of shots overlaid with a deep, videogame-like voice announcing the start and end of each 90-second round. To assume control over one of the twelve guns, players can purchase five credits for $5 and proceed to aim at round targets that turn from green to red when struck. I was given the opportunity to take the macabre game for a spin prior to launch, and was left scratching my head. After a brief 30 seconds inside a waiting room, I assumed control over the turrets, click on green targets spread out across a simple “arena” decked out with motorcycles and mannequins that the guns seemed to refuse to fire at. “Range hot, real gun turrets, click to shoot green targets,” the booming hypermasculine voiceover announces. “Most kills win.” The gun turrets are powered by “extremely high pressure air, not gunpowder,” Shov founder Domm Holland told Futurism an email. In other words, in many ways the project is basically an online carnival shooting gallery. Upon closer inspection, you can see hoses connected to each of the guns feeding them round ammunitions. “We had them manufactured for us, they are primarily hardened rubber,” Holland told Futurism, referring to the projectiles and sharing a photo of a sign dented by one of them. “But extremely hard.” The game also technically doesn’t need any humans to shoot the targets. “Any empty seat gets an AI player, marked [AI] — so every round is a full 12-turret firefight,” a tiny notice at the bottom of the company’s website reads. As such, a panel on the right shows the live players actively firing at targets. Prior to launch, each listed player was accompanied by a suspiciously well-lit selfie, exactly the kind of AI-generated profiles we’ve repeatedly come across while reporting on the slopification of the internet. Personally, I feel reassured that I wasn’t actually controlling a lethal weapon, something I’m not planning to do any time soon. For now, I’ll happily keep the gunfire limited to video games — a far better value proposition than clicking green targets inside a browser. Meta Now Banning Ads That Promote Feminism

Futurism | 2026-09-21

Visit Original Website
For years, human rights monitors have been outraged at the selective policing of content on Meta’s social media platforms like Facebook and Instagram. The company’s latest enforcement action is hardly shocking at this point, but it provides a crystal clear example of the kinds of things Meta will — and won’t — allow on its apps. In a bizarre case of digital censorship, Meta’s content moderation system stepped in to stop a local theatre in Barcelona from promoting a stage adaptation on the grounds that it violated a rule on promoting “social topics,” per the Guardian. The production in question is an adaptation of “A Room of One’s Own,” a book by the legendary English author and feminist Virginia Woolf. According to the Teatre Raval, planned promotional posts about the show were blocked on both Instagram and Facebook, likely by an automated content moderation system. After reaching out to Meta about why the promo campaign had been stomped out, the company replied that “any mention of civil rights, feminism or social reform are usually classified as ‘social topics’ by our automatic system,” and therefore rejected. According to the Guardian, the company then cryptically recommended the Raval remove “key words” related to social activism, so the promo campaign can pass the automated censor — though the company failed to explain what, exactly, its content system objected to. “We don’t know if the publicity has been rejected because of the name of Virginia Woolf or because of the word feminism, or both,” Clara Sanchis, who stars in the one-woman show told the Spanish newspaper La Vanguardia, per the Guardian, which translated the comments. “In any case, it’s a way of erasing from the map advances that have taken centuries to achieve,” Sanchis continued. “We have given social media unlimited power and every day we can see how dangerous this is. It’s bias and manipulation.” The automated rejection and the ensuing apathy from Meta come after some galling incidents where Meta refused to take down content significantly more radical than a theatrical performance about a literary figure whose books are read by high schoolers. In August, Futurism reported that Meta was refusing to take meaningful action against a far-right influencer campaign to drum up support for a “crusader march on Dearborn” Michigan, organized by self-described “anti-Arab” extremist Jake Lang. Though Lang’s initial post wasn’t “sponsored,” meaning he didn’t officially pay to promote his call-to-action as the Teatre Raval was trying to do, his post nonetheless garnered thousands of reactions, comments, and shares from his 274,000 followers. It also spawned a deluge of copycat posts extolling racism and Islamophobia, some of which contained explicit calls for violence which Meta refused to take down. And earlier in September, Meta was found to be hosting a massive number of AI-generated videos depicting horrific acts of child abuse. Meta removed a tiny handful of videos after Futurism approached the company for comment — but refused to act on others, leaving the vast majority of violent AI Reels untouched. In all, Meta has drawn a revolting line in the sand — with nominally progressive “social topics” on the verboten side, and base depictions of torture and calls for violence allowed sight unseen. Hundreds of Flights Grounded Across the Eastern Seaboard as Government Rolls Out AI Air Traffic Control System

Futurism | 2026-09-21

Visit Original Website
Hundreds of flights across New Jersey, Philadelphia, New York City, and Boston were delayed Monday afternoon after the US Federal Aviation Administration issued a massive ground stop impacting thousands of travelers — right as the government starts testing an AI air traffic control system, though there’s no current indication that the two incidents are connected. According to early reporting by Reuters, over 600 flights were delayed across Newark and Philadelphia alone, in addition to hundreds more across four other east coast airports. Dozens of imminent takeoffs were likewise aborted across the busy flight corridor when the ground stops — which appeared to roll northward from Philadelphia — were issued. Preliminary reports indicate some sort of air traffic control glitch at one of the major airports along the East Coast, with Bloomberg TV reporting a circuit malfunction at an air traffic control facility in Philadelphia, and Reuters noting that a fiber optics cable was accidentally clipped in New Jersey. At the time of writing, it isn’t exactly clear what’s behind the turmoil. What is clear is that the region’s air traffic control system is a tangled mess — and unfortunately, this could just be a sign of things to come. Strikingly, the massive air traffic outage comes right as the Trump administration moves ahead with a controversial AI air traffic control system known as “Strategic Management of Airspace, Routes and Trajectories,” or SMART. According to further reporting by Bloomberg, the federal government is “soft launching” the program this week across the Washington DC region, where it’s now dolling out AI-generated recommendations to flight traffic personnel. As part of the rollout, SMART will reportedly be installed in a number of East Coast airports, like Reagan National Airport in Virginia, and Baltimore/Washington International Thurgood Marshall Airport in Maryland, though it’s not known which facilities have officially gone live with the AI tool. Any connection between the massive ground stop and the SMART soft launch would be speculation at this point, and early reports don’t seem to indicate a connection between the two events. What is clear is that air traffic control is in dire straights across the United States — though the American public has yet to encounter any federal system which a hallucinating, error-prone AI assistant can’t make significantly worse. It Turns Out the US Military Really Did Use AI to Blow Up a School Full of Iranian Children

Futurism | 2026-09-21

Visit Original Website
Now over half a year into the United States’ war on Iran, it’s becoming abundantly clear that AI has been instrumental to the American campaign. On the home front, AI and its infrastructure is quietly shaping the ideological battlefield: President Donald Trump is spewing out wartime propaganda slopped together by generative AI, as US-owned data centers and tech company offices become widely accepted as military targets. AI is also proving instrumental for helping US Central Command choose targets for obliteration via drone strikes or missiles — so much so that an AI system played a pivotal role in perpetuating an American war crime. According to new reporting by Bloomberg, the Pentagon’s investigation into the February 28th air strikes on the Shajareh Tayyebeh girls’ school — a double-tap strike which killed over 150 people, including at least 123 children — concluded that the attack was the result of outdated intelligence and over-reliance on an AI targeting system. Those errors, stemming from dusty intel, seem to have been compounded by the involvement of an as-yet unidentified AI targeting system, likely similar to the one used by American military officers to fast-track air strikes on over 2,000 Iranian targets within a four-day window in the early days of the conflict. The latest info directly refutes president Donald Trump’s own narrative around the deadly incident. In July, Trump downplayed the United States’ responsibility for the attack, even suggesting that images and footage of the massacre could have been generated by AI. Yet as a UN fact-finding commission wrote in its report last week, there are “reasonable grounds” to conclude the US attacks amount to “war crimes” — the girls’ school attack being just one prominent example. Whether the full Pentagon investigation will ever be made available to the public is unclear. What is certain is that killer AI is already here — and it doesn’t need to be sentient to help humans claim hundreds of innocent lives. Behold: Video of “First-Ever” Human Versus Robot MMA Fight

Visit Original Website
We’ve seen humanoid bots pull off impressive martial arts choreographies, run faster than the world’s fastest person, tear it up on the dance floor, and face off in the ring against each other. But — at least beyond a few humans catching stray blows from robots in stunts gone awry — we’d been yet to see a robot contender square off in the ring against a homo sapien fighter. Until now, that is. A new video making the rounds on social media shows humanoid robot fighting company REK have one of its androids spar with influencer — and major TikTok personality — Frankie LaPenna, who’s known for, among other things, stuffing his shorts to make it look like he has a huge behind. (LaPenna is such a big deal that he recently appeared on NBC‘s “America’s Got Talent,” where it was ultimately determined that he did not.) “For the first time in history, a human faces off against a six-foot-tall robot,” an announcer exclaims in the video. “Dude, I’m scared for you man,” one of the match officials tells LaPenna with a laugh. The 44-second clip shows the heavily padded influencer take swipes at the imposing robot inside a plastic-lined octagon — but to no avail. The brief match has LaPenna getting repeatedly kicked in the chest and midsection, only to be thrown across the ring with one final kick, seemingly ending the fight. (The robot, in a theatrical twist, had been outfitted with a terrifying “Terminator”-like head.) While we can certainly see at least some entertainment value of having daring human athletes square off with machines, there’s clearly something off with the match. For one, LaPenna clearly struggles to land a proper blow, suggesting he may be pulling his punches and eventually throwing the fight. And whether the robot really has enough force to throw LaPenna across the entire ring with a simple kick is debatable at best. “Is this just a showcase or did that robot legit yeet that dude five meters across the ring?” one Reddit user wondered. “It’s still clumsy… for now,” another added ominously. Besides, the stocky, 28-year-old influencer is well known for playing the heel in public and is willing to throw himself the line of fire for comedic effect. He recently has shared videos of himself crashing a bike into an office desk, jousting while standing on a boat, and even playing a game of cards in the vicinity of a raging bull. In other words, the jury is still out on how an actual MMA fighter would fare against a six-foot robot. But considering what they’re going up against, we’d hazard a guess that it wouldn’t exactly be much of a fair fight. For one, humans bruise a whole lot easier than a construct made out of metal and plastic. More on fighting robots: Scientists Create New Humanoid Robot That Flinches in Fear When You Come Close

Signs of Crisis at Flock as It Offers Employees Buyouts to Quit and Get Off Payroll
2026-09-21

Signs of Crisis at Flock as It Offers Employees Buyouts to Quit and Get Off Payroll

Futurism | 2026-09-21

Visit Original Website
As backlash against its surveillance network refuses to die down, Flock Safety is kicking off an employee buyout program that’ll let workers leave the company with a “generous” severance package, Wired reports. It’s seemingly an admission that the company is in turmoil. Protestors have targeted Flock’s AI-powered license plate scanning cameras so fiercely that many cities and police departments are cancelling or declining to extend their contracts, which could leave the enterprise short of revenue goals, according to Wired‘s sources. It’s either buyouts for the 1,500 worker-strong company, or layoffs. Employees who want to leave are getting a solid deal, at least according to Flock. The voluntary severance program, which started accepting applications Friday, gives employees several months of health care coverage and the ability to exercise stock options within two years of separation. Some employees have reportedly said they’ve received buyout offers in the tens of thousands of dollars. Flock, in its professed munificence, called the buyout package as the “most generous” ever offered by the company, extolling that it provides “greater agency” for its employees. There’s no word yet on how many people have applied or will be accepted, but applicants are expected to hear a decision on October 9. Most of those who get accepted will leave by October 29. Sources familiar with the program said they expected that a significant number of employees will leave. It’s not just the overwhelming backlash against the company that could push employees to exit. There are also rumors that the company might sell off parts or all of its business. “When you see enough of the vandalism,” one of the people told Wired, “you know the writing is on the wall.” An analysis by the advocacy group Secure Justice found that at least 214 cities and counties have dropped their Flock contracts since 2021 — and 90 of those cancelled or lapsed contracts came in 2026 alone. In August, an average of three local administrations were cancelling their Flock contracts each day. Because of the purpose of Flock’s services — conducting mass surveillance — these cancellations have created a vicious cycle. The more municipalities that its cameras go down in, the less useful that Flock becomes to its remaining customers. Some police departments have explicitly cited the loss of coverage from neighboring towns cancelling their contracts as a reason to do the same. When Flock was confronted with the cancellation data less than a month ago, a spokesperson confidently responded that renewals were outpacing cancellations. But if the buyout program is any indication, there’s a lot more anxiety about its future behind the scenes. Singapore’s Nexstrom wants to bring 2D semiconductors to chip fabs
2026-09-22

Singapore’s Nexstrom wants to bring 2D semiconductors to chip fabs

TechCrunch | 2026-09-22

Visit Original Website
Disrupt 2026: OpenAI, Anthropic, Replit, and more take over 6 industry stages. 25% off tickets now Disrupt ticket savings of up to $200 + 50% off a second ends Sept 25, 11:59 p.m. PT. REGISTER HERE. For decades, chipmakers have sought to make semiconductors faster by shrinking down silicon transistors and packing more of them on each chip. But shrinking transistors comes with its own problems: controlling electron flow becomes harder, which exacerbates problems such as leakage, power loss and heat. Nexstrom, a semiconductor startup incubated by Xora Innovation, thinks using two-dimensional (2D) materials instead of silicon is one viable way to solve that problem, especially as the global component shortage driven by demand for data centers shows no signs of letting up. The Singapore-based company is building equipment and processes that would let foundries manufacture extremely thin semiconductors, known as transition-metal dichalcogenides (TMDs), directly on chip wafers. Now, TMDs aren’t really two-dimensional; they’re just so slim that they’re bunched together with a large family of materials that are called “2D materials” to illustrate their thinness. TMDs are just a few atomic layers thick, a feature that has drawn attention from chipmakers seeking ways to further shrink chip sizes. The startup says its product, called North Star, is designed to grow TMDs directly on 300mm (12-inch) wafers used in commercial chipmaking. The startup isn’t alone in recognizing the potential of using 2D semiconductors to shrink chips further. TSMC, Intel and Belgium’s IMEC are all developing ways to integrate 2D materials into transistors, and equipment and materials companies such as AIXTRON, CDimension and several startups are tackling other parts of the manufacturing chain. TSMC, ASML and IMEC recently even showed how 2D-material transistors could be used in a 300mm integration process. Nexstrom, however, is betting that it can make strides in a particularly difficult area: producing these materials for semiconductor production at scale. The company’s technology is based in part on research by its co-founder and chief scientist Lance Li, who previously led research on post-silicon electronics at TSMC. Li has worked on 2D materials since 2012. “In the lab, people can get very small, high-quality materials, but only on a very small scale,” Li told TechCrunch. “This is exactly the gap Nexstrom is addressing.” To do that, the Singapore-based company on Tuesday said it has raised a $12 million seed round from investors including Xora, Foothill Ventures and SEEDS. The company has so far raised $15 million in total. But instead of competing with chipmaking giants like TSMC, Samsung and Intel, Nexstrom wants to sell its technology to them or work with them as partners. The company says some companies in the industry are already testing samples of its wafers and materials, though it declined to name them. Still, there are several problems to be solved before such tiny semiconductors can be manufactured at scale, chief of which is consistently growing TMDs across a full-size wafer. “At the current stage, our work is focused on delivering material quality that [industry partners] have specified,” Nexstrom’s CEO Phoebe Tan told TechCrunch. “We have proven material results on wafers using our 12-inch system that has just been installed. We have systematically scaled from 2 inches to 6 inches, and we are going to complete our 8 inch and 8 inch by the end of October.” Nexstrom plans to use the funding to further develop its technology and move its equipment closer to commercial production. Tan said the money will also go toward improving process control and measurement capabilities, expanding its team, and supporting qualification programs with potential customers. The startup expects its equipment to be ready for commercial production between 2030 and 2035. When you purchase through links in our articles, we may earn a small commission. Bill Gates’ Breakthrough Energy bets these 21 startups will shape energy’s future

Bill Gates’ Breakthrough Energy bets these 21 startups will shape energy’s future

TechCrunch | 2026-09-22

Visit Original Website
Disrupt 2026: OpenAI, Anthropic, Replit, and more take over 6 industry stages. 25% off tickets now Disrupt ticket savings of up to $200 + 50% off a second ends Sept 25, 11:59 p.m. PT. REGISTER HERE. While the world is focused on the power demands of AI data centers, the folks at Breakthrough Energy are paying attention, but not for the reasons you might think. “Data centers are a 10% bump against basically flat demand for electricity over the last 30 years. So it looks like a big bump, but data centers are absolutely the canary in the coal mine,” Eric Toone, managing partner at Breakthrough Energy Ventures, told TechCrunch. “You’re chasing a coming massive increase in demand for electricity.” That demand is forecast to surge as countries electrify entire sectors of their economies to rein in carbon emissions. “Electrifying everything implies a 3x increase in demand for electricity,” Toone said. In other words, the coming decades will make today’s scramble for power seem quaint by comparison. To help prepare for that future, Breakthrough Energy has spent the last several years supporting a handful of early stage startups with its Breakthrough Energy Fellows program. This year’s installment, its sixth, features a range of companies and technologies spanning everything from agriculture and nuclear fusion to robotics and data center power efficiency. TechCrunch received an exclusive look at the incoming cohort. This cohort of fellows is the first announced since Ashley Grosh, who previously led the Fellows program, departed to become a partner at WovenEarth Ventures. In the meantime, senior director Benjamin Gaddy is serving as acting director while the organization determines what’s next, Toone said. Despite Grosh’s departure, this cohort still hews to the formula that defined previous years. “We’re always always right on the line that you’re not going to fund things that are silly, but the name of the game here is to take some big bets and hope to get some big payoffs,” Toone said. Some of the incoming fellows certainly qualify as big swings. Among them is Borealis Fusion, a startup that’s pursuing fusion power using proton-boron fuel, which is widely available, but challenging to heat to the temperatures required to ignite fusion reactions. Despite the long-shot nature of proton-boron fusion, the potential upside is significant. “If they can do it, you could get to much lower cost of energy,” Gaddy told TechCrunch. Another fusion startup, StarWarden, is working on heat management technology for future reactors. Other startups, including Bedrock Semiconductor, K1 Semiconductor, and Vari Flux, are working on ways to squeeze more efficiency out of semiconductors and data centers. K1, for example, is developing a new, more efficient method to process key semiconductor materials, including gallium nitride and silicon carbide. “You could reduce the cost of these power electronics by maybe 50%, maybe more,” Gaddy said. Breakthrough Energy is also placing bets on agriculture and critical minerals, including molecular sensing techniques to detect crop disease and robotics systems to harvest minerals from the ocean floor while minimizing disturbance. It’s also supporting several startups, including AmyHyTech and Ammovolt Energy, that are developing ways to use and transport hydrogen with ammonia. “Hydrogen is the Swiss army knife of energy. If you have enough hydrogen, and importantly if its cheap enough, you can do anything,” Toone said. But the infrastructure to move it around doesn’t exist. “There’s pretty close to zero installed capacity in this country,” he said. “There’s no simple way to move hydrogen around, and ammonia offers a potential way to do that.” But for all the startups focused on decarbonization, this year’s fellows class has a number that are working to help humanity adapt to a warmer climate. “I think that folks really hoped that we were going to be able to decarbonize faster than we were able to,” Toone said. “It’s going to take longer and it’s going to be harder than we thought, and that absolutely leads you directly to adaptation.” When you purchase through links in our articles, we may earn a small commission. Everyone can find a reason to dislike data center construction
2026-09-22

Everyone can find a reason to dislike data center construction

TechCrunch | 2026-09-22

Visit Original Website
Disrupt 2026: OpenAI, Anthropic, Replit, and more take over 6 industry stages. 25% off tickets now Disrupt ticket savings of up to $200 + 50% off a second ends Sept 25, 11:59 p.m. PT. REGISTER HERE. Frontier AI labs are convinced that scaling up computation is the path to improving artificial intelligence, and they’ve put their money where their mouths are, to the tune of trillions of dollars of planned data center projects. The rest of us? Surveys of public opinion say that more than 60% of Americans favor limiting new data centers, particularly in their communities. And the longest-running surveys show that sentiment is growing fast. Data center projects worth $68 billion were disrupted by local opponents in the second quarter of 2026, according to the trackers at Data Center Watch. AI companies and their investors have a hard time reconciling the growing demand for their products with opposition to the key infrastructure behind it. Some answers from the industry, like the idea that data center opposition is a product of secretive Chinese meddling, don’t really pass the smell test. Today, a new report from the nonprofit Data & Society offers a deeper look at what is activating opposition to data centers. It is the result of 18 months of ethnographic fieldwork by a team of researchers who interviewed 44 Pennsylvanians between 2024 and 2026. The authors, who are critical of the way AI has been pitched, aren’t trying to measure the empirical impact of data centers, but instead want to understand the way people experience the debate over their construction. “One of the strengths of ethnography and doing research on the ground for an extended period of time is piercing the bubble of AI hype,” Livia Garofalo, a Data & Society researcher, told TechCrunch. “People are making sense of the dissonances between AI being the next inevitable industrial revolution, and fears about AI.” When the authors began their work, data centers were being pitched as key to economic development. Governor Josh Shapiro headlined a July 2025 summit celebrating $90 billion in data center and AI infrastructure investment. A year later, Shapiro signed an order imposing new requirements on data center projects and removing them from a regulatory fast-track initiative. Someone read the writing on the wall. The authors say the message from industry that AI is “inevitable” has landed poorly with Pennsylvania residents. They point to the state’s historic waves of industrialization, including coal mining, the early days of the oil industry, steel manufacturing, and fracking, experiences that have left residents suspicious of utopian industrial schemes. “People in Pennsylvania especially have incredible experience with industrial change, have deep lineages of industrial trauma, enough to sniff when things don’t feel right to them,” Maia Woluchem, a program director at Data & Society who co-authored the report, said. Still, trade unions are perhaps the greatest source of support for data center projects, thanks to the potential jobs they represent. Even acknowledging that, once built, these data centers won’t be major sources of employment, unions see the projects as a lifeline to rebuild their ranks. In contrast to the industry’s fears of foreign influence, the researchers describe the work of AI boosters as a kind of propaganda campaign, pointing to the Data Center Coalition, a trade group that has set up websites like “Pennsylvania Connects” to boost projects and spent at least $19,000 lobbying the state government last year. In spite of the boosterism, resistance has grown, and in unlikely ways. The researchers said they were surprised by the diversity of the opposition to these projects, calling it not bipartisan but post-partisan.“We don’t want to romanticize that everyone is having this kumbaya moment, [but AI is] forcing people to be in conversation together about futures that they want and presents that they want … these fights are incredibly organic,” Woluchem said, pointing to a variety of sources of opposition: Sometimes it is simply folks who are against development — classic NIMBYs — but the coalition grows from there. There are people who dislike AI as a technology and the corporate interests behind it, environmental groups concerned about water usage and energy-related emissions, and those with more nebulous concerns about public health. What’s striking, though, is how often material interests are top of mind. People are concerned that data center electrical usage will affect their own power bills, worried that data centers will drive down property values, or are upset by the playbook that data center builders use to get their way — tactics such as asking local officials to sign non-disclosure agreements. “There is a significant group of people who are just really clued into their town hall meetings and might notice that wow, a representative from Amazon, Meta, Microsoft, showed up here, or a data center developer showed up at my community meeting …that’s a little odd,” Woluchem notes. “And my council person, who I have previously had a relationship with, now won’t talk to me about this sort of thing.” It’s this patchwork quilt of motivations for anti-data center activism that presents such a challenge for the AI industry: There’s no simple way to alleviate this raft of concerns. The problem is trust, which has already deviled the industry. “It is an intimate and relational trust that is now being inserted in people’s lives by these industries,” Garofalo said. “I’m alone in my house, and I’m talking to a chatbot, and I’m feeling loved and cared for, and yet I know that this is a commodity, and I know I’m being extracted upon.” When you purchase through links in our articles, we may earn a small commission. Nscale’s IPO will test Wall Street’s appetite for concentrated AI bets once again

Nscale’s IPO will test Wall Street’s appetite for concentrated AI bets once again

TechCrunch | 2026-09-22

Visit Original Website

When British neocloud Nscale goes public, it will test public investors’ appetite for a stock whose revenue is tied primarily to two customers. Since it was spun out of Australian cryptocurrency mining company Arkon Energy two years ago, Nscale has amassed over $103 billion worth of contracts, according to its IPO filing. But there’s a catch: Most of that, about 85%, comes from a deal to supply Microsoft with $43.8 billion worth of compute through 2033, and another supply agreement worth $44.6 billion with Anthropic. Moreover, Anthropic’s agreement is contingent on Nscale obtaining financing, and the AI lab retains the right to walk away from or cancel the deal if Nscale fails to hit milestones that the filing explicitly categorizes as “stringent.” Nscale’s customer concentration is a reminder of just how interconnected the AI industry has become. A recent paper by credit hedge fund Sona Asset Management, featured in Financial Times, found that many AI infrastructure providers heavily depend on a limited number of customers. Nscale’s competitor CoreWeave, for example, generates 67% of its revenue from Microsoft, and data center builder Applied Digital derives 67% of its revenue from Oracle, and 30% from CoreWeave. While Sona noted that such interconnectedness is not necessarily a bad thing, it pointed out that a single setback or strategic shift by a major player can easily affect the entire industry. Nscale, which plans to list on the NYSE, expects to be valued at $35 billion, Financial Times reported, and is seeking to raise $3 billion in the offering, according to Bloomberg. The company reported revenue of $140.6 million for the six months ended June 30, up significantly compared to $10.4 million a year earlier. Net losses jumped to $1.02 billion from $369 million in the same period. Earlier this month, one of Nscale’s major investors, Nvidia, agreed to provide the company with $1 billion in convertible debt as part of a larger $3.1 billion financing deal. The startup was valued at $14.6 billion when it raised a $2 billion Series C led by Aker ASA and 8090 Industries. Aside from CoreWeave, Nscale’s competitors include Nebius, Lambda, and Crusoe — the latter last week said it raised $3.9 billion at a $30.9 billion valuation. Nscale operates data centers in Norway, Portugal, Texas, and West Virginia. Its board of directors includes former Meta executives Sheryl Sandberg and Nick Clegg, as well as former OpenAI executive Fidji Simo. Meta deal will help plastics recycling startup MacroCycle build its first factory
2026-09-22

Meta deal will help plastics recycling startup MacroCycle build its first factory

TechCrunch | 2026-09-22

Visit Original Website
Disrupt 2026: OpenAI, Anthropic, Replit, and more take over 6 industry stages. 25% off tickets now Disrupt ticket savings of up to $200 + 50% off a second ends Sept 25, 11:59 p.m. PT. REGISTER HERE. Plastics recycling startup MacroCycle announced a new deal with Meta on Tuesday that will help speed the development of its first commercial plant. Three-year-old MacroCycle, based in Cambridge, Massachusetts, has developed a novel way to process plastic waste that strips it of contaminants, making the resulting recycled material more attractive to customers. The startup says its technology generates 80% fewer carbon emissions than new, non-recycled virgin PET plastic (a common type of plastic used in bottles, containers, and textiles). MacroCycle was a Top 20 finalist in the 2025 Startup Battlefield competition at TechCrunch Disrupt in San Francisco. Under the terms of the agreement, Meta will pay MacroCycle for the rights to those avoided emissions, which lets Meta count the reduction toward its own carbon footprint, which is growing dramatically as a result of the AI boom. A Meta spokesperson confirmed that this is the first such deal the company has signed. MacroCycle gave TechCrunch an exclusive preview of the news. Payments for what are known as environmental attribute credits (EACs) will provide MacroCycle with a revenue stream that can help support the construction of the new plant, which will be built in the U.S. Meta’s interest goes beyond the credits themselves. It hopes to help create a market for low-carbon materials like plastics, which are used in its supply chain for things like packaging and hardware. As that market grows, broader access to those materials should help lower the company’s overall carbon footprint. MacroCycle says its demonstration plant will be capable of producing 5,000 metric tons of recycled plastic per year. The company’s process works by dissolving and purifying PET that’s present in a range of waste streams, including textiles, which have one of the lowest recycling rates of all materials at 0.5%. The startup’s process loops plastic polymers back on themselves, creating rings called macrocycles, which is where the company gets its name. Solvents wash away contaminants, leaving the macrocycles behind. The loop is then opened, and the polymers can be linked together to form higher-quality plastics. The result is a material that’s indistinguishable from new plastic. MacroCycle’s use of solvents instead of heat is a major source of its energy savings, which should also result in lower costs, too. The company’s goal is to produce recycled textiles domestically at prices that compete with those from overseas suppliers. The U.S. textile manufacturing industry has been hollowed out over the last 25 years, with employment in the sector down 85%. MacroCycle is currently working to secure buyers for the material that its first plant will produce. The deal with Meta should make subsequent agreements with other companies easier to obtain, MacroCycle co-founder and CEO Stwart Peña Feliz told TechCrunch. Future plants, he added, will be capable of producing 50,000 metric tons of material per year. When you purchase through links in our articles, we may earn a small commission. Morphotonics raises €40M to expand its display tech into data centers
2026-09-22

Morphotonics raises €40M to expand its display tech into data centers

TechCrunch | 2026-09-22

Visit Original Website
Disrupt 2026: OpenAI, Anthropic, Replit, and more take over 6 industry stages. 25% off tickets now Disrupt ticket savings of up to $200 + 50% off a second ends Sept 25, 11:59 p.m. PT. REGISTER HERE. The Netherlands’ ASML is possibly the biggest name in lithography, using its machines to etch patterns onto silicon wafers inside a computer chip factory known as a fab. Fellow Dutch company Morphotonics is smaller and works in a different corner of the same field: nanoimprint lithography, or NIL, a process used to manufacture the displays in AR glasses and other optics. It said it has benefited from ASML’s supply chain and uses many of the same vendors. Morphotonics has spent 12 years developing that technology, largely under the radar. Now, as demand for smart glasses and other optics surges, the company said Tuesday it has raised €40 million to scale up production and push into a new market: optical components for data centers.The round was backed by 3M Ventures, Innovation Industries, BOM, and Invest-NL. The European Innovation Council (EIC) Fund; Ernij Next, a Dutch family office; and the European Investment Bank (EIB) also participated. Morphotonics’ NIL work means the startup creates a “stamp” and applies it to photosensitive materials to create various kinds of displays, from augmented reality glasses to privacy screens for cars. This is useful in creating the waveguide technology used in smart glasses, including the Meta Ray-Ban Display, Even Reality, and Magic Leap. Waveguides are thin pieces of glass or plastic that deflect light along a specific path so that they display an image in front of the wearer’s eyes. Smart glasses are just one application of Morphotonics’ technology, but it’s currently the most in-demand one. This week, China said sales of smart glasses in the country doubled in the first eight months of this year. In June, the market research firm IDC noted that shipments of smart glasses with displays are expected to reach 12.2 million by 2030. As that market grows, manufacturers are looking for machines like Morphotonics’ that can produce those displays at scale. The latest fundraising is part of an extended round that began in 2024, raised in multiple installments. The company has used the money to more than double its headcount to 60 employees, up from roughly 30 in September 2024, when CEO Hugo Da Silva joined. Morphotonics still plans to hire and expects headcount to stabilize around 70 to 75 people. “Typically, display manufacturers would acquire our equipment as part of the supply chain, and we integrate it into the entire manufacturing process. We teach them the process, we provide the chemicals, and they can manufacture that,” Da Silva said. The company’s manufacturing is heavily concentrated in Asia, with teams in China, Taiwan, South Korea, and the U.S. “Strong local presence is very important,” he said. Da Silva said the company’s next machine, currently being built, will be capable of producing more than 6 million waveguides a year. He said it’s expected to start shipping early next year. Beyond displays, that expansion also includes a push into co-packaged optics, a technology used in data centers that use Photonic Integrated Circuits — chips that use light instead of electricity — to move data between servers and network equipment. The startup hasn’t shipped any machines in this area, but it said customers have validated its tech. The company sells both hardware machines and the process through licensing to ensure that customers get the best results. Right now, roughly 90% of the startup’s revenue comes from hardware sales, with 10 to 15 systems deployed globally. The company expects to reach 50 deployments in the next two to three years, which should also increase its revenue from services. The Past Decade’s Emissions Are Already Driving Worse Heat Waves in Europe
2026-09-22

The Past Decade’s Emissions Are Already Driving Worse Heat Waves in Europe

TIME | 2026-09-22

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings A woman shelters from the sun with a parasol on June 21, 2026 in Seville, Spain. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings The heat waves Europe has seen in recent years were made measurably more intense because of the greenhouse gasses emitted since the 2015 Paris Climate Agreement, according to a new study published today in Geophysical Research Letters. “We found that the emissions released since the signing of this Paris Agreement in 2015 have substantially and significantly increased the intensity of Europe's recent individual heat waves,” says lead author Jared Trok, a PhD student in Earth system science at the Stanford Doerr School of Sustainability. The study also determined that that held true for Europe’s hottest week in every individual year since at least 2021. Their findings come on the heels of this summer's back-to-back heat waves on the continent, which broke heat records and contributed to at least 35,000 excess deaths. Read more: In Europe, Today's Children Will Live Through Nearly Twice as Many Fire Weather Days Than Their Grandparents The Paris Agreement was a legally binding international treaty on climate change adopted by nearly 200 countries in December 2015, with the aim of limiting global warming to 1.5°C above pre-industrial temperatures. A decade later, the world is expected to blow past that 1.5°C target, with optimistic estimates finding that temperatures will peak at 1.8°C under current climate policies. Beyond the 1.5°C  threshold, every extra fraction of a degree matters. The world can expect to see intensified extreme weather, ecosystem loss, and flooding in coastal cities and island nations, along with far-reaching impacts to human health and food security. Europe is particularly vulnerable in the face of a warming climate. The continent is heating up at more than twice the global average rate. The June and July 2025 heat waves broke records across the continent—only to be surpassed by 2026 heat waves. Surface air temperatures exceeded 40°C (104°F)  in several countries, and up to 46°C (114.8°F) in Spain and Portugal. The researchers created a generative AI model known as a diffusion model using simulated weather data from 10 global climate models to predict how extreme heat waves in Europe could have unfolded under different global emissions levels. “This is a purpose built machine learning model that we trained on large amounts of climate model data…to make predictions about how individual heat waves might have unfolded differently if those same weather patterns had occurred under different levels of human emissions,” says Trok. The team gave the model the observed weather conditions that caused Europe’s most intense annual heat waves from 2016 to 2025, which typically coincided with a persistent, high pressure system known as a heat dome. They had the model predict daily temperatures under emission levels at the time of each extreme heat event, and under the lower emissions levels of 2015. They found that the emissions from the last decade increased the intensity of 2025 heat waves by roughly a third of a degree Celsius. “That third of a degree might seem very small, maybe imperceptible to us walking around, but there's a large body of scientific literature that suggests that even small increments of temperature on a hot day can have these disproportionate impacts on the damages caused by those heat waves,” says Trok. Scientists have long known that the burning of fossil fuels trap greenhouse gases in the atmosphere, raising global temperatures. In recent decades, researchers have developed methods to study how historical emissions have affected heat waves, hurricanes, droughts, wildfires, and other extreme weather events. The researchers say that the methodology represents a step forward for climate modeling. “This research shows that we can systematically and objectively ask questions about subsets of historical emissions, including individual heat waves,” says co-author Noah Diffenbaugh, professor at the Stanford Doerr School of Sustainability. “A key part of what we've identified here is we've been able to say with high confidence that this subset of emissions has shifted the distribution of temperatures that could occur during this individual heat wave to such an extent that we can say we are very confident that this individual heat wave would have been cooler if the same weather patterns had occurred without this subset of emissions.” It also serves as a warning, says Diffenbaugh, noting that the emissions that have already been released will impact our climate regardless of any mitigation efforts we take going forward. “Even in the context of the most optimistic, ambitious future for reducing emissions, there's still more intensification of extremes that is essentially committed.”
