The Press Is Not the Root of Trump’s Problems
2026-09-21

The Press Is Not the Root of Trump’s Problems

The Atlantic | 2026-09-21

Visit Original Website
This is an edition of The Atlantic Daily, a newsletter that guides you through the biggest stories of the day, helps you discover new ideas, and recommends the best in culture. Sign up for it here. President Trump is going through a rough patch—in foreign relations, in the economy, in the courts, and in election outlook—and he believes that he has found the culprits: CNN, MS NOW, and Politico. On Friday, Trump said he was banning the three outlets from the White House “as a result of their constant ‘reporting’ FAKE NEWS” and warned that more bans could come. When he feels beleaguered, he tends to lash out at familiar enemies, especially reporters. The idea that negative coverage is to blame for his predicament is perversely flattering. But Trump’s true scourge is reality, not how the news is covering it. Although the decision is part of a gradually intensifying attack on free speech, as I noted last month, it has drawn particular pushback. This morning, five major TV networks (including Fox News) agreed to suspend pool coverage of the president and White House events after CNN was blocked from its previously assigned spot in a pool rotation today. The three banned outlets also sued over their exclusion. How such a suit will fare is unclear; Trump banned the Associated Press from the White House press pool last year after it refused to adopt his renaming of the Gulf of Mexico, and a lawsuit challenging the ban is still pending. After Trump committed a Kinsley gaffe on Friday by calling his move “the ban on the free press,” he tried to clean up his spin this morning. “The White House is not instituting an assault on the Free Press, something which I cherish. It is instituting an assault on the FAKE NEWS,” he wrote, claiming that it “is a threat to our National Security, and must be stopped, NOW!” This is nonsense. Trump has every right to hate the way he’s covered, but if the government has the power to decide what is fake and what is true, then the press is not free. Trump’s post demonstrates his talismanic belief in the power of news coverage. The president himself has been obsessed with attention—how to get it and how to shape it—for his entire life, and he is a relic from an era when the media were so dominant, and had such control over the means of reaching an audience, that they seemed to shape reality. They are much diminished today, occupying a strange, unstable role: strong enough to be blamed for both Trump’s rise and his downfall, yet weak enough that they can be threatened with relative impunity. Though Trump’s attacks on free speech are unlike any in living memory, his is not the first administration to believe that its problems are about messaging and coverage. In 2012, Barack Obama said that the biggest mistake of his first term “was thinking that this job was just about getting the policy right,” because “the nature of this office is also to tell a story to the American people.” A decade later, Joe Biden’s administration was convinced that negative coverage of inflation and Biden’s age were bigger problems than inflation or the president’s evident frailty. Now Trump is making the same misjudgment. He is not unpopular because of Politico, an outlet that focuses largely on Washington machinations, or because of MS NOW or CNN, two cable channels that—as he is fond of pointing out—have relatively small audiences for television. The media cannot be “failing” and all-powerful at the same time. Trump is unpopular because (to name a few big reasons) he is losing a war of choice to Iran, making inflation worse without seeming to care how voters feel, overreaching with draconian immigration enforcement, and focusing on vanity projects that don’t matter to voters. Ironically, Trump is committing the same error as many liberal critics of the press, who seem to believe that popular support for the president would have collapsed long ago if the media had used harsher words to describe him. But the idea that tougher coverage would have stopped him is fantasy. Although the press has made many mistakes in how it has reported on the president, including giving him hours of uninterrupted TV air time in 2016, downplaying bigoted remarks, and “sanewashing” some statements, Trump has also received more intensive investigation and negative coverage than any president in American history. Some of that coverage has surely been hackish partisanship—which is protected by the First Amendment too—but much of it has been earned. Newspapers, magazines, wire services, and TV networks have turned out a series of scoops about the chaos inside the administration, Trump’s policy errors, and the pervasive corruption around him. In late 2023, The Atlantic published a set of prescient essays considering what would happen if Trump won back the White House. Voters had even lived through one Trump presidency already. They had access to the relevant information before they voted him in again. Believing that Americans might have chosen differently if only The New York Times had abandoned its obtuse headline conventions is comforting to some Trump critics, because it puts the blame on the press rather than acknowledging that a majority of those who voted wanted Trump. By the same token, Trump would rather blame news outlets he does not like than confront the reality that some of those same voters are rejecting him because of his choices. The president faces an additional risk in trying to ban these three outlets. He detests adversarial coverage, but he desperately desires attention. This afternoon, Trump spoke with reporters outside the White House. Usually, his comments are captured by the boom mics from the pool TV team, but without their presence today, he was inaudible. Censorship can silence anyone, it turns out—even the censor. Here are four new stories from The Atlantic: When I was 20 years old, I started to sense a hole inside me. The feeling was utterly concrete, though the result of no medical condition that any of the various doctors I saw could identify. The hole felt like a viscous emptiness—a paradox, admittedly, as any real emptiness should have felt like nothing. This was a nothing, but its nothingness very much felt like something—and something that was growing … My friends found my behavior disturbing. I was missing class in college, showing up to theater rehearsals drunk. A caring young woman I was dating could take no more of my savage, ranting mood swings and persistent despair. She broke up with me over tea at the student union and implored me to seek help. I knew she was right. Even sleep was now fraught with violent, terrifying dreams—of rocket ships out of control, conflagrations and invasions, and, yes, holes into which I would disappear. But exactly what sort of help I might need, I didn’t know. Listen. James Parker compiled 50 songs that explain America. Investigate. The Babydust Method features advice debunked decades ago about how couples can select their baby’s sex, Olga Khazan writes. Why have tens of thousands of people bought in? Stephanie Bai contributed to this newsletter. When you buy a book using a link in this newsletter, we receive a commission. Thank you for supporting The Atlantic.

The Confrontation That Broke New York
2026-09-21

The Confrontation That Broke New York

The Atlantic | 2026-09-21

Visit Original Website
In the spring of 2023, a young Black man stepped onto a subway car in Manhattan. He screamed, threw garbage at passengers, and announced that he was prepared to die. New Yorkers often encase themselves in psychological armor on the subway, but for many of the riders in that car, this was too much. Terror flooded in. A woman hid her face in a friend’s chest; a mother tried to hide behind her stroller with her toddler. One man began to pray. Then a 24-year-old white man, an ex-Marine, grabbed the screaming man and put him in a neck lock, and five minutes later—by mistake? Intentionally?—left that man dead on the floor of the subway car. To read the new book on that killing, Man in the Mirror: Hope, Struggle, and Belonging in an American City, by Anand Giridharadas, is to recognize that the membrane separating safety and fear on the New York City subways appeared in danger of rupturing by 2023. In the preceding few years, dozens of people had been pushed onto subway tracks, suffering injury or death. Murder and assault had risen sharply. The tension between chaos and order—often evident in New York—could be acute. And in those five minutes, it snapped. Before anyone could sort out the facts, too many people rushed to impose narratives. Jordan Neely became the homeless Black man with the too-disposable life; Daniel Penny became the muscular vigilante who grew up in the white-bread suburbs. Leftist activists howled racism; right-wing sorts adjusted their ties and talked tough. “In a civilized society, the strong protect the weak,” a member of The Wall Street Journal’s editorial board wrote. “A Marine uses his strength to fight for those unable to fight for themselves.” Liberal politicians divined morality tales. Brad Lander, at the time the city comptroller, who lives in a prosperous liberal precinct in Brooklyn, proclaimed: “We must not become a city where a mentally ill human being can be choked to death by a vigilante without consequence.” I wanted to say: Stop, please. Forgo reading your political catechisms. Try not to talk of consequences and militarization and the menace of the streets until we have a clue about how to prevent this from happening again. There is pathos enough to be found in basic facts. In his book, Giridharadas takes on the considerable challenge of telling a story that encompasses conflicting narratives and the voices of nearly every player: defense attorneys, people living on the street, public-health officials, both Neely and Penny. His writing is most powerful when he traces the threads of tragically entwined lives. Whereas some in the media reduced Neely to a trope—the mentally ill homeless man—Man in the Mirror invests his life with complication and nuance. His mother was killed by her boyfriend; in a gothic twist, that boyfriend cross-examined young Jordan while representing himself at his trial. In time, Neely drifted further and further away from those who wanted to offer him succor. The reader learns about outreach workers and prosecutors who were ambivalent on how to treat his illness, and about well-meaning bureaucrats whose actions fell short. Read: The heart of the debate over Jordan Neely’s death Here and there, the author stumbles when inserting liberal punctuation points. He argues that addressing crime is an inescapable fact of life for Democrats, even if the fear that crime is out of control is “based in nonreality.” The biggest problem, he writes, is a nation “awash in anxiety and fear: of change, of the social advancement of marginalized populations, of losing control of children, of losing control of the border, of losing cultural status and centrality, of the Other, of chaos.” This strikes me as too easy. Change, demographic and otherwise, has swept through and revitalized New York City for centuries. And the subways are arteries that give life to us all. I grew up riding the rails to see girlfriends and basketball games. My schoolmates and I rode to a Bronx public high school in train cars so encased in graffiti, we had to peer out of the opening doors to see if we’d reached our stop. Slowly, however, that system grew cleaner and safer. When the coronavirus pandemic overtook New York City, that momentum seemed to reverse, and the subways began to feel menacing again. My claim here is reality-based, drawing on statistics and personal experience. Assaults on the subway increased by 65 percent from 2020 to 2025, and the number of murders in the system spiked as well. And a year after Neely died, a man shoved a friend of my family onto the subway tracks. He suffered multiple fractures, and his rehabilitation stretched across brutal months. One morning in the spring of 2023, I was riding the Q train to the Upper East Side when I heard a booming voice and saw a great block of a man enter the subway car and ask for money. I returned to my reading. As the man approached, the timbre of his voice grew agitated. I fixed a side eye on him. When the train stopped in the middle of a tunnel, the panhandler stood over a young man who sat across from me. He put out a massive hand. “You’re giving me money,” he announced. Silence followed as the young passenger kept eyes downcast. “Mutherfucker, I’ve got a big-ass knife”—the man patted his shaggy overcoat—“and I’m going to gut you from your balls to your throat.” I made eye contact with other men on the train. What the hell do we do if he pulls out that knife? If we are lucky enough to pull him to the floor, how do we keep him there? I offer none of this to sound macho. I’m a 60-something man with a strong desire to remain alive. I harbored no illusions about my ability to wrestle this man to the ground. As it happened, the train started and pulled into the next station and he got off, but not before loudly vowing to gut us all. I can venture a reasonable thumbnail sketch of this man. He was probably homeless. He was likely known to social-services caseworkers and to police. If he had been prescribed medications, he did not seem to be taking them. His inner resources had been eroded by life’s dysfunctions and the brutal reality of too many days on the street. In a 2024 column about Neely and Penny in The New York Times, Emmett Rensin noted that the prevalence of “madness”—“those serious and spectacular forms of mental illness”—on the streets and in the subways “creates a dilemma between two principles of a liberal society: the sovereignty of individuals and the collective right to public order.” When those values can’t be reconciled, the city can feel perilous. I have a desperate desire not to live in a Death Wish version of New York, in which residents take it upon themselves to mete out justice, but what happens when you are caught in a moral pincer without a third party to adjudicate? Had that man on the Q pulled a knife, and had we hurt him before he hurt us, would we have been branded vigilantes? The worst of those times appear to have passed. Murders and shootings in New York City have plunged in the past few years, and it remains one of the nation’s safest big cities. But statistics comfort only to a point. The continuing presence in public of so many people with seemingly untreated mental illness—including those who still push strangers onto the tracks—is unignorable. To read Man in the Mirror is to inoculate yourself against the belief that the larger crisis is over. Giridharadas’s book illuminates the conundrum posed by those with Neely’s behavioral profile. On a good day, he could be a gentle busker. He was the Michael Jackson impersonator with a moonwalk step and a sweet smile. But Neely’s mental illness most often went untreated, and it had festered as he evaded caseworkers. He was becoming dangerous. In 2019, he punched someone on a subway platform. Two years later, he slugged a 67-year-old woman as she emerged from a subway station, breaking her nose and fracturing her eye socket; for this, he served a sentence at Rikers Island. Locking away the Neelys of our cities in Kafkaesque asylums was a more common solution until the 1960s and ’70s; the practice was ultimately rejected for urgent and humane reasons. Yet discerning which mentally ill person poses a threat has not gotten any easier, and letting the most afflicted roam the streets untreated does not constitute a sustainable policy. Giridharadas writes sensitively about this, and illuminates debates between hopeful public-health officials who yearn to transform society—changing “everything ‘out there’”—and hardheaded pragmatists. The latter group supports a sensible program in New York, now in its early stages, to pair public caseworkers with police officers, marrying the compassion of the former with the practical authority of law enforcement—the better to offer the right kind of help in any situation. There is also an effort to make it easier for families to commit mentally ill relatives for care. Unfortunately, when Giridharadas moves away from the man who died in the confrontation and the questions that arise from the incident, his analysis grows less subtle. He devotes substantial pages early in his account to the Black activist Al Sharpton without mentioning the extent to which the reverend is a sometimes-unreliable narrator. Sharpton can speak movingly of the lives of the Black forgotten and of those who suffer from police abuse; he also has served as an FBI informant, been on a first-name basis with mobsters, and embraced an outright hoax about a racist conspiracy. And in his politics, he is in some ways an outlier. Many Black New Yorkers, as well as Latinos and Asians, have told pollsters that they favor more, not less, police presence, and that safer subways are an urgent concern. That perspective could have enriched the book. The author also succumbs in places to class-based stereotypes, particularly when writing of Penny and his family of white suburbanites, people who are unlikely to share his political priors. Such missteps limit rather than expand our understanding of Penny’s background and worldview. Penny grew up in West Islip, on Long Island, a town where, Giridharadas writes, “you had your little yard, your little fence, your little deck, maybe you had your little boat.” He adds that “nothing was beautiful and nothing was ugly.” He casts a cold eye on Penny’s mother, Gina, mocking her working-class accent and writing that she had “so much to express” on Facebook, “but not necessarily the skill or will to express those things in any personal, authentic, or original way.” The book reaches a crescendo with Penny’s trial. Defense lawyers portrayed a city lost to dysfunction; the police were too late to show up, and only Penny was left to put his life on the line to save “perfect strangers.” The prosecutor—who several times during the trial referred to the former Marine as “the white man” and “the white defendant”—acknowledged what activists and liberal politicians could not: Penny had “started out trying to do the right thing,” until he kept his choke hold in place too long and Neely died. A majority of the jurors were white, but at least four were not. They took 24 hours—not very long, given the case’s complexity—to acquit Penny of criminally negligent homicide. Afterward, Lander warned that the verdict would “valorize vigilantism.” The left-wing Working Families Party called it a “modern-day lynching.” But all of the jurors rode the subway at least semi-regularly; several had spoken before the trial of their own upsetting experiences on the train, and in the end they appeared disinclined to punish a man who acted to forestall what might have been imminent violence. It is a virtue of this book that Giridharadas, even while seeming to harbor sympathies for the prosecutors, applies an empathy for New Yorkers who try to thread their way through the divides created by this case. “They shared the sense of being undefended. They shared the sense that the calvary was not coming,” he writes. “They shared a sense that the system was rigged,” and so “shared the sense of their own powerlessness.” Just hours after the verdict, Penny walked along 30th Street, and people recognized him. “Almost to a person,” Giridharadas notes, “they shook his hand and thanked him.” It’s tempting to think of this, three years after the incident, as a snapshot of a more turbulent city. But as I finished this essay, I learned of a new horror: Early this month, Erin Piacenti was walking through Times Square, having freshly returned from maternity leave, when Pamela Cisneros, a middle-aged mother of three who appeared lost to madness, fatally stabbed Piacenti in the abdomen. Cisneros, who had suffered decades of mental-health issues, then tried to stab police officers; they shot and killed her. And for Piacenti, the young, innocent woman who lost her life, no one was there to save her. ​When you buy a book using a link on this page, we receive a commission. Thank you for supporting The Atlantic.

What Were My Dreams Trying to Tell Me?
2026-09-21

What Were My Dreams Trying to Tell Me?

The Atlantic | 2026-09-21

Visit Original Website
When I was 20 years old, I started to sense a hole inside me. The feeling was utterly concrete, though the result of no medical condition that any of the various doctors I saw could identify. The hole felt like a viscous emptiness—a paradox, admittedly, as any real emptiness should have felt like nothing. This was a nothing, but its nothingness very much felt like something—and something that was growing. Check out more from this issue and find your next story to read. Accompanying this feeling of an expanding hole at the center of me was a sense that I was losing more and more of myself to it, like shoreline eroded by rising waters. To lose myself felt less like oblivion than instability. I observed in myself a new susceptibility to the opinions of others, and a new recklessness, as if some dark, decisive action might either quiet my doubts about my fundamental worth or end my longing once and for all. I recall one particularly dangerous, drunken night that had me well over the legal limit, both aware that I was getting into a car that could kill me and others, and also not believing that any of it mattered. The onset of my devolving well-being was strange and sudden, and seemed connected to childhood in some way I couldn’t understand. I hadn’t been a tortured child but rather placid, if introverted. My parents always said that I had been easy to keep happy. It sometimes seemed to me as if the hole I was experiencing was one where some piece of my childhood had once resided. My friends found my behavior disturbing. I was missing class in college, showing up to theater rehearsals drunk. A caring young woman I was dating could take no more of my savage, ranting mood swings and persistent despair. She broke up with me over tea at the student union and implored me to seek help. I knew she was right. Even sleep was now fraught with violent, terrifying dreams—of rocket ships out of control, conflagrations and invasions, and, yes, holes into which I would disappear. But exactly what sort of help I might need, I didn’t know. I confided my woes to a friend who’d grown up on a farm in Kentucky. He never missed Sunday Mass, and when I told him about the hole inside me, he pulled a copy of The Seven Storey Mountain from his bag and handed it to me. Thomas Merton, he said, had written about something similar. It was what had led him to leave the world and become a monk. I found deep resonance in the book’s harrowing account of Merton’s time at Cambridge and Columbia—the intense restlessness and emptiness, the disgust and torpor and despair. But Merton’s solution was theistic: a turn to God that I could only read as naive and distorting. I didn’t have any interest in God. Even the passages that approached the specificity of my own experience were charged with personification that read as anachronistic at best: The room was very quiet. It was rather dark, too. And as I lay in bed, in my weariness and pain and disgust, I felt for a moment the shadow of another visitor pass into the room. It was death, that came to stand by my bed. I kept my eyes closed, more out of apathy than anything else. But anyway, there was no need to open one’s eyes to see the visitor, to see death. Death is someone you see very clearly with eyes in the center of your heart: eyes that see not by reacting to light, but by reacting to a kind of a chill from within the marrow of your own life. I knew this experience: a central chill, a lightlessness connected to the doomed feeling of some ultimate end. But I resisted any characterization that departed from the fundamental sensation itself—of a growing hole inside me. For no reason I knew, I was convinced that remaining honest with myself about this basic fact was the only thing I could do to help myself. My parents were worried. They flew out to visit me at school and figure out what was wrong. Over a meal they prepared for me in a hotel room with a kitchen, I confided in them. There was a hole inside me that I needed to address, I said. They were understandably perplexed. My mother found me a psychiatrist, an avuncular Brit who was more interested in my sex life than my interior void, which I insisted was more real than he seemed willing to allow. But something important endured from the handful of sessions I had with him. A volume on the shelf over his left ear drew my fascination, and would soon fill weeks of my life: Freud’s The Interpretation of Dreams. That book began a process in me, what I’ve often called “curiosity” when I’ve told this story, but which is probably better described as a form of relationship. The nightly tumult of disturbing dreams persisted, and even if I couldn’t be sure whether Freud was right about how to make sense of them, simply attempting to do so seemed to help. Paying attention made it easier to remember my dreams, and to remember more of them. In one, I was carefully drawing an empty circle on a page of the Quran that my grandparents had given me as a boy, a circle through which—impossibly—I could reach and touch cool water. I awoke with the feeling that the hole inside me might be leading to something better, however unknown. Even so, it wasn’t until I mentioned that I was interested in dreams to a teacher I admired that I started to keep a detailed nightly log. This teacher had done a great deal of dream-work, and suggested that I tie a pencil to my finger to remind me, in those brief moments of stirring between sleep cycles, to write down whatever fragments were stirring in hypnagogic consciousness. So I did. What resulted was a daily haul of interior riches. The vividness of my unadulterated hopes and sorrows, the unusual expansiveness and wonder I experienced by night, lived on in the often-paradoxical images I would recall throughout the day. My study of Freud led to a similar scouring of the work of other psychoanalysts—and, later, mystics such as Emanuel Swedenborg, Ibn Arabi, and Rabbi Nachman—for clues about what kind of sense to be making of my dreams. In my final year of college, I encountered, in a late work of Carl Jung’s, the advice to treat dreams as meaningful psychic events, a technique to facilitate what Jung called their “compensatory” function—balancing the biases and limitations of one’s conscious attitudes. I took this to mean treating a dream’s images as real in their own right. Whether doing so had any such balancing effect on me, I can’t be sure. But my persistent despair seemed to be dimming; the hole inside me felt different the more I was able to gaze patiently into it for whatever was coming through. The dream-work was paying dividends, though what exactly was paying them out, I had no clue. I know how what I’m about to share is likely to come off; it isn’t the first time I’ve spoken it aloud. I started to dream of things that hadn’t happened yet, to “see” things at night that then appeared in my life during the day. One of the earliest and most startling of these uncanny doublings took place in my first year after college. I was living and working in a small town in the Tuscan countryside, without a phone, before the internet. I dreamed that a friend I knew in Paris showed up on my doorstep with skis on his back. Two days later, I awoke to the sound of my name being called from down in the piazza, outside my front window. I opened the worn balcony doors to find the very friend I’d dreamed of; he’d gotten my Italian address from a mutual friend and seen that the town I was living in wasn’t too far out of the way as he made the trip from Rome up to the city in the Alps where he planned to spend a few days skiing. I resisted speaking of what had happened, lest I be impelled to name whatever in me had seized my bewildered attention. The Tuscan episode was not the last time dream and day seemed to cross wires. Years later, I would dream of a terrorist attack on Manhattan two days before 9/11, and of a malign, cough-inducing fog slowly engulfing the world several weeks before the eruption of the coronavirus into my (and the world’s) awareness—improbable coincidences I struggled to accept as such. And yet, these visions became the occasion to doubt any picture of the universe that failed to make room for what could only be experienced as a kind of magic. If the prognosticating mode had been the only benefit to accrue from my attention to my dreams, I might not have been so diligent in my recordkeeping. After all, I wasn’t seeing stock prices or lottery numbers, the only rationale many might have accepted for my habit of destroying every good night’s rest. For at that point, I was waking up every 45 minutes to reach for the bedside pad on which I made notes about whatever was moving around inside me. But my night work was opening up creative vistas. One mentor, well-versed in working with the unconscious, suggested that it was enlarging the space of what she kept calling—despite my confusion about what she really meant by it—my psyche. Indeed, a growing spaciousness was now my sense of the interior core where the hole had opened up. The hole itself wasn’t gone, but it had changed character, less a wound now than an opening, though I couldn’t always tell the difference. And yet, the trajectory seemed to be shifting. That pliant, receptive brightness bearing the imprint of something more than just my name, the self, began to feel heightened, enlarged. I had learned that some cultures conceived of the self as containing two parts: a smaller, individual, daily self, and a larger, multiple, primordial one. This greater self, it was believed, was the one that dreamed. Something about this way of thinking felt more useful—and more reflective of my experience—than any of the latest studies in neuroscience I read about the physiological functions of dreams. Psyche : Greek for “soul.” Which felt like a more appropriate frame of reference—despite my continued resistance to the language of theism—for experiences that so evidently defied the materialist doctrines of my late-20th-century education. To privilege the Newtonian model of classical mechanics—one object pushing or pulling another, all the way down to the molecules that formed consciousness—over the actual data of my experience felt like intellectual laziness. I understood that others would see my willingness to abide more patiently in the mystery as irrational. My usage of the Greek term started to give way to its English corollary. The word soul seemed to make better sense of whatever was expanding inside me, a rising that was also a deepening, married to the uncanny knowing of what seemed like prognostication, and, more valuably, to a natural empathy for others. And in case this comes off as more altruistic than seems reasonable: Less blinded by myself, more porous to others, I found, too, that I was becoming a better writer. From the November 2024 issue: Jeffrey Goldberg, Ayad Akhtar, Robert Downey Jr., and Bartlett Sher on writing in the age of AI Soul was, for me then, a mode of being mostly experienced in images by night that shaped my understanding by day—for example, that a close friend was like the abandoned dog I dreamed he was, long neglected by his family, never allowed into the house; an image that helped me navigate his touchiness, providing readier access to the wellspring of loving loyalty that coursed beneath his habitual choler. I found in the use of soul a capaciousness that not only accounted for this richer relationship to others and the world—and to myself—but served as a bridge to deeper mystery, and to a new patience that allowed me to acclimate to it. There were other benefits, too. Just as the vital, vivid stuff of my brain’s nightlife enriched my being—to use a word that joined soul in the lexicon of descriptive terms of dubious provenance—so did my immersion in dream dross seem to feed my capacities with language. There was a stronger tether now between word and thing, a tether, paradoxically, that operated simultaneously in many directions and layers. The webbed, protean mode of associative thinking—one thing reaching for another, in opposing vectors at once—was less an effort than a reflex, more instinctive than the ordinal fixity of my usual habits of mind. Shakespeare, whom I’d long struggled to apprehend, now made easier sense, even as the experience of reading him felt like perpetual entry into undiscovered country, the phonetic expatiations, the dense semantic clusters, the sounds and images opening up more and more effortlessly into a particular state of absorptive flow, a pleasing emotional and intellectual static that I took to calling “linguistic space.” I started taking a yoga class on the Upper West Side. Our becalming teacher spent the better part of our weekly hour teaching us to meditate. Setting aside my irritation with her hushed and holy tones, I found myself growing more susceptible to her instructions. I could catch a gap between thoughts that felt curiously similar to a rich blankness that resulted from hours spent in linguistic space. The fixed point of focus on the breath sharpened my mind and filled it with something closer to light than thought. On my own, I began to sit—sometimes for hours—in this pregnant blankness, exhibiting a facility for what the yoga teacher called “practice” that I couldn’t explain. My weekly hour with that yoga teacher sparked an interest in meditation that led to weeks, and years, of working with other instructors of inner techniques. I learned to visualize and hold in memory shapes derived from Gnostic practices and colors derived from Sufi ones. Intoning Abulafia’s list of Hebrew names for God at the proper pitch and volume shook the bones in my ear and nerves along my neck. Calm was promised, and often followed, though I wondered whether this said more about my susceptibility to suggestion than the practices themselves. Sometimes what followed was stranger. Once, as I sat in a darkened room at dusk, observing the breath going in and out of me, I felt that breath begin to breathe itself. In the half shadows, with my eyes closed, a startling brightness waxed and waned with the movement of air. What was this light? I wondered. Was this just the room’s faint light filtering through my eyelids, brightening and dimming as my pupils dilated and contracted with each breath? If so, was this suggestion too? I would come to no definitive answer. The inner light continued to strobe. If it was all suggestion, I didn’t mind. It was getting me further than faith had. From Meister Eckhart I learned to recognize in my meditative blankness a silence clean of longing, one that, if I could remain in it, would release an astonishing vitality. Others I studied with found it strange that my interest in meditation would be piqued by dream-work and quickened by pentameter. They sometimes remarked that what I was up to resembled anthropology more than devotion. They were only partly right. To me, it was all a study in being, or soul, or spirit (the latest addition to my lexicon)—which is to say, an inquiry into the substance and nature of the interior life. My reading, too, reflected the scavenging of a magpie in search of material from which to build its nest: the Phaedrus and Patanjali and Mulla Sadra; Being and Time one month, Mathnawi the next. What exactly I thought I was doing was not a matter of concern as long as I kept to myself about it. What I’m compressing into paragraphs here took so much longer, of course, and was hardly as sequential as telling makes it sound. I kept at it for the reward of the work itself. I had experiences of rapture (and sorrow) so deep, of pleasures so unprecedented, it was sometimes hard to return to the dishes and the rent. But I had studied with a remarkable Vedic teacher who warned me about seeking states rather than the truth. “What is the truth?” I once asked him as we sauntered along a Tennessee creek. He pulled his orange robe tighter against a rising spring wind. He was too smart to oblige. “The truth is not like knowledge about that tree,” he said, pointing to a blooming magnolia. “Only the tree knows its truth.” I didn’t want to pretend I understood him. “You write stories,” he said. “Inside the story is a meaning that is the reason for it. Not words that carry it, but the wordless substance of its truth you take away.” Despite my customary reticence about such things, I had occasions to compare notes with fellow travelers. One evening, at a fundraising function downtown, I found myself sharing martinis with a physician whose lived-in eloquence about his Zen practice felt like an opening. I was not expecting the lesson in brain science he proceeded to impart: I’d become addicted, he said, to activating certain parts of my cerebral cortex. Like an addict—I flinched at that word—I was pulling on neurological switches, training my brain to release its own pleasure chemicals through a growing grab bag of inner tricks. Clearly he didn’t approve. I was too embarrassed to ask why, even as I recalled my teacher’s warning not to chase states, but rather to seek the wordless substance of truth. I resisted the physician’s account even as I couldn’t ignore the unease it stirred in me. My fellow traveler was right, at least as far as neuroscience went. Studies were beginning to show that meditation—a loose term for the various practices of inner focus I was involved with—activated and deactivated specific parts of the human brain. In the 1990s, the neuroscientist Andrew Newberg studied advanced Tibetan monks and Franciscan nuns as they entered deep meditative states. The results were strikingly similar. In both groups, areas of the brain believed to be responsible for orienting us in space and time showed markedly decreased activity, as if the neural basis for the boundary between self and other had gone quiet. They were using very different techniques, but the results, neurologically speaking, were nearly indistinguishable. Each group, however, described the experience differently: the nuns as a feeling of union with Christ, the monks as a state of emptiness. I knew enough about Buddhism to recognize in that word, emptiness, something very different from the hole that had opened up inside me during early adulthood. For the Buddhist, perception of the nature of reality discloses the identity of emptiness with form, and form with emptiness. It’s a perception described in other traditions by other means, and sometimes, in Christianity, as an annihilation of the individual personality in the presence of God. The maps and words and techniques were different; somehow, the underlying experience itself, beyond words or ideas, was the same. Not quite in the words of my Vedic teacher: The brain scans were a story containing a truth known only to the one inside them. For a long time, all of this felt like little more than a motley, life-enhancing assortment, which is not to downplay the valuable place it had in my life. Rightly or wrongly, I attributed the return of my fundamental stability after the harrowing years of early adulthood—and the return of the placid, placable self I’d known much earlier in life—to my commitment to daily practice. I would come to think of inner work as an adult corollary to the joyous absorption of childhood play. I’d found a way to play again, I concluded, and somehow this had seemed to “fill” the hole again. But the life of the spirit was not what I took my real life to be. I was a writer. I wanted to know the broken world that was, not the healed one that could be. I had no fundamental interest in escaping the human condition. To the extent that spirit—or soulfulness—anchored my curiosity, quickened my perceptions, deepened my empathy, I rationalized its creative and analytical utility. In truth, something less tangible than optimization bound me to practice. It kept me in the company of more eternal conundrums, questions of consciousness and origin and meaning for which, of course, I expected no answer. But proximity to the unknown—living contact with it, if you will—seemed like a worthy form of knowledge. In this, I was like many friends who took psychedelics; I wanted to recover the awe and mystery I recognized in their accounts when they shared them. For the most part, though, I was still doing what my teacher had warned me against—chasing states. In part, this was a function of resisting meaning. I had been too respectful of my teacher back then to tell him I wasn’t sure that a story’s meaning mattered to me the way he assumed it would. But I felt the slow accretion of a wordless substance inside me, not well-being or certainty or the rapture of some fugitive sublime, but something I might call intimacy: intimacy with an inwardness spied only indirectly, whose purer acquaintance I seemed to be making very slowly indeed. With the hindsight of decades, I can track a tectonic shift away from the visible to something more diffuse and sustaining, an interior through line that has both warned me against the tyranny of acquisition—of things, of states—and provided the means of finding sustenance in absence. Whatever this is, whatever name I might give it, I can’t deny that it all seems to have proceeded from that perception of a hole I felt or imagined when I was younger. William James might have called it a “sick soul”; I still don’t feel entirely confident about what word to use for the continuity that I’ve found or constructed, though some word has always been necessary. I had spent years resisting every vocabulary that exceeded the raw data of sensation, only to find that this austerity, too, had to be exceeded. Too much of what mattered most in my own life had remained unspoken not because it was unreal, but because I did not know how to bring it into language without betraying it. For decades, I steered conversations away from these inner experiences, obedient to the rule that such things could not be talked about without sounding foolish or deranged. But my reticence began to feel like capitulation to falseness. It took me years to understand that my silence was not only protective but deforming—for without others to hear it, the still, small voice within was so much harder to discern. And yet, the silence was not my own to break. With so little room in our common life for such things, we have no common language for them either. Ralph Waldo Emerson knew the problem. In “The Over-Soul,” he wrote that language could not paint this “subtile” order of our experience in its own colors; but he knew how important it was to keep trying. The great Russian novelists knew it too, treating soul not as a word reserved for Sundays, but as the moral substance of life. I’ve found myself mourning not their metaphysics but their seriousness, their acceptance of the inscrutable boundlessness of the inner life—unruly, morally consequent—as a subject for public speech. What matters to me now is less the defense of any particular term than the search for a language in which such things can be spoken of seriously, in fiction and elsewhere, without piety or diagnosis. Without a language for speaking openly together about the inner life, what often matters most to us remains locked away, inaccessible to the inquiry and challenge vital to all knowledge. As I finished a draft of this essay, I had a dream. In it, I stood at the edge of a well—another hole, I realized as I made note the following morning—where there was no bucket to draw water. Around it moved countless people in a flurried hubbub, shepherds and masons and architects, all of us trying to figure out how to draw the water whose surface we could see in the faint shimmering at the bottom. It was going to take time, I thought, before we figured it out—just as I awoke. The Authors Guild confirms [authorsguild.org] HarperCollins' $5,000-per-title arrangement, split equally with participating authors. Crucially, it was opt-in, and the author's payment did not count against their advance. The Guild also criticized the publisher's 50% share. Wiley confirms $49 million in fiscal 2026 AI revenue [q4cdn.com]. That establishes a market; whether authors are getting a good deal requires looking at the contracts. PW reports approximately 15 other author-led lawsuits and roughly 10 publisher suits [publishersweekly.com]; those remain the publication's estimates, rather than an independently reproduced case count. The 63% figure comes from survey respondents reporting their employers' AI use [publishersweekly.com]. It does not measure how much publishing work AI performs. The certification paragraph needs the most care. Human Authored explicitly uses identity verification and contractual self-certification [authorsguild.org]. It does not scan a manuscript and prove that a human wrote it. Meanwhile, Created by Humans describes itself as an AI rights licensing platform [createdbyhumans.ai]. Listing these services together should not imply that reliable authorship detection is a solved problem.
