Trump Posted About Magic Beds That Cure Cancer. Millions of Americans Believe They’re Real. I Found the Guy Who Started It All.
2026-09-22

Trump Posted About Magic Beds That Cure Cancer. Millions of Americans Believe They’re Real. I Found the Guy Who Started It All.

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-22

Visit Original Website
The video that sent me spiraling looks like a Fox News report. There’s President Donald Trump in a screen within a screen, sitting at the Resolute Desk. He smiles broadly as he signs an executive order. Then, b-roll, panning above a vast two-wing building identifiable as a hospital by its helipad. Next, an interior shot, sweeping across a gleaming white room occupied by sleek screens and glass-domed beds, space-age Sleeping Beauty contraptions. “Breaking now,” Lara Trump, the president’s daughter-in-law, says from what appears to be a Fox News studio, “President Donald J. Trump has announced a historic new healthcare system: the launch of America’s first medbed hospitals and a national medbed card for every citizen.” I saw this video the night Donald Trump posted it for his 10 million–plus followers. I watched it several times, genuinely shocked. The president of the United States had promoted one of the cruelest conspiracy theories on the internet: the dark fantasy of medbeds. The first thing to know about medbeds is that they aren’t real. Instead, they are the subject of an imagined plotline in which the military or some group of elites has access to extraordinarily advanced technology that can quickly and painlessly cure the human body of anything. In this fiction, peddled by right-wing conspiracy-theory influencers, the end to all disease and disability will arrive when Trump defeats his enemies and releases medbeds to the public—one of the many rapturous delights promised for that day. This conspiracy theory operates on a doomsday preacher’s ever-shifting timeline: The medbeds are always on the horizon, or being released imminently, or secretly already out there. There’s no good gauge of how many people believe in medbeds or Trump’s ability to deliver them. But a critical mass keeps the theory alive and rocketing around the internet. Trump’s post, I realized, had marked its entry into mainstream politics. Trump’s account deleted the video the morning after posting it. (We reached out to the White House several times for clarification on why but received no response.) But the damage had been done. This was September 2025, and A.I. videos were getting more convincing by the day. There was a telltale uncanniness to it—people clapping too slowly, as if underwater—but there was no disclaimer that it was fake. More to the point, the president had posted a video of himself speaking to the camera to make the announcement. It was easy to see how a viewer might think the report was real—or at least a nod to something real that was coming. At the time, I wrote a short article explaining that medbeds were another disturbing idea to come out of QAnon, the conspiracy theory that promises that Trump is secretly plotting to expose and arrest a cabal of evil elites trafficking children and poisoning society. I wrote about the president’s recklessness in giving this specific conspiracy theory his stamp of approval. After all, if someone thought a miracle cure was right around the corner, they might be more likely to stop taking their medication or skip burdensome treatments. The piece received a decent number of readers, and a couple of radio shows reached out to have me on to talk about Trump and his supporters who believe in medbeds. Then the internet, and everyone else, moved on. But I did not. I couldn’t stop thinking about medbeds. I found myself increasingly stuck on how nonsensical it all seemed, that such a patently absurd idea—magic beds!—could find its way to the most powerful person in the world. How, I wondered, had this happened? Had anyone involved in perpetuating it stopped to consider that it wasn’t harmless? That some people might stop taking insulin or pursuing chemotherapy, or that other desperate people would be given false hope? More than with any other conspiracy theory I had ever encountered, I needed to know who was responsible. What followed was a nearly yearlong obsession that, for stretches, took over my life. The search for the source of the medbed conspiracy took me into the world of scammers and crystals and alien invasions and broken families and sci-fi movie sets and the Arizona desert. I corresponded with con artists and attended a strange “medical” appointment that promised me invisible surgery and extraterrestrial matchmaking. I discussed lizard people and societal collapse with true believers. There was an entire month when I couldn’t stop talking to my editor about UFO discussion boards. I had grim conversations with many people about the horrors they’d seen after their loved ones fell into the world of medbeds. Somewhere along the way, I realized that my search for the origin of medbeds was about more than this one conspiracy theory. It was about how we came to such a dark moment in American life. In the end, I got answers—both about the origins of this specific conspiracy and about much more. And, after digging through layers of internet ugliness, I found the last thing I expected: a reason to believe we might just make it out of our current mess. It’s been about a year since Erika Looney decided she needed to cut her mother off. The moment came while she was driving to pick up her 8-year-old son, who has a developmental disorder. The school had called; he had hit another child. And then, sitting passenger in her Honda Accord as they went to get him, her mother said something that crossed a line: “It’s just a matter of time, and he’ll be completely fixed.” Erika knew exactly what her mother meant: Stop worrying, the medbeds are coming soon. She had heard it many times before, particularly on her son’s bad days. Erika understood why her mother, Bambi, would want medbeds to be real. It wasn’t just for her grandson. Bambi had lost a foot in a tractor accident when she was a child. But this time, Erika couldn’t bear it. Because of a rare chromosomal condition, her son will require full-time care for the rest of his life. With the way his disorder manifests, Erika’s family can’t do something as simple as go to the movies. She knows not to hope for a cure. And that day, Erika was too worn out and too upset to be patient anymore. “I flipped out, and I said, ‘I’m sick of this shit,’ ” Erika recalled. “I just shut down. And I think that’s really the moment I started blocking her out.” After that day, Erika stopped talking to her mother, even when she encountered her in her house. In May, she finally told her mother to move out. I heard a lot of stories like this as I dove into the world of medbeds. I found some of them in discussion forums. Other times, I was interviewing believers and, more often, their bewildered loved ones. People told me about parents who resisted treatment for cancer; a father-in-law who almost died of sepsis after neglecting a urinary tract infection; a father who, on his deathbed, complained that the medbeds weren’t there to save him; and an uncle who died after postponing medical care, sure that medbeds would soon arrive. These believers had, like so many conspiracy theorists before them, started from a place of legitimate suspicion of powerful institutions. They merely pushed further than the average internet truth seeker. On Facebook or Truth Social or X, even in texts from friends, they read about suppressed medical devices. They grew curious. They followed those threads to Facebook groups and Telegram channels. They were often, by this point, already convinced something big was coming that would upend our social order, if not our entire understanding of the world. And so, when they ended up in front of miraculous testimonials of healing, often supplemented by A.I.-generated photos or videos, they took it seriously. Now they crowdsource questions about when the beds will come and how they will work and argue over which medbed whistleblowers are real and which are frauds. They try to decode world events, looking for clues that the promised day of “awakening” has arrived. For Bambi, it was during the pandemic that she suddenly began talking about medbeds. Not long after, she started going on about Democrats eating babies. Later, Erika would realize that medbeds had been Bambi’s entry point into the world of right-wing conspiracy theories. Bambi, a woman in her late 60s, began consuming conspiratorial media so religiously that she set timers to make sure she didn’t miss podcasts. Eventually, she stopped taking her medication for high blood pressure and diabetes. Her blood sugar levels grew perilously elevated. A baffled Erika felt that her mom had been replaced with a combative and paranoid stranger. But to Bambi and those like her, medbeds are a source of comfort. The idea reassures believers that true patriots like themselves will soon be financially secure, respected, and healthy—and it also offers more immediate relief too. It provides a sense of community and the thrill of holding forbidden knowledge. In the Facebook and Telegram channels, believers share their stories of physical suffering and cheer each other on. Often, they post about loved ones with terminal diseases, trying to calculate whether the medbeds will arrive in time. “Any day now!” people write in the comments. They discuss what they’ll do first once they regain their vitality. Some fantasize about running around with their grandchildren. The daughter of another medbed believer recalled her mother’s words: We won’t have those aches and pains anymore; your dad’s heart will be better. “What’s heartbreaking,” the daughter told me, “is the hope.” But that hope, it turns out, is also profitable. The reply came within hours, and it was good news: With a refundable $500 deposit, I could secure an appointment with a medbed specialist in D.C. “Welcome to the Med Beds Technology Registration Department, where we facilitate access to cutting-edge quantum medical technology for health restoration and wellness enhancement,” the email read. In the forums, I had noticed some accounts insisting that medbeds were already being rolled out. Official-sounding email addresses floated around, offering bookings and stringing people along before asking for large payments. I reached out to an account I found in a Telegram chat that offered a medbed appointment. The reply came from a purported government agency, “the National Quantum Coordination Office.” I asked questions about the technology and how the process would work; it was firm in saying I could get answers only at the actual appointment, which I could reserve with a Venmo payment. I had found this email, and others, through accounts affiliated with a figure known as “Skye Prince.” There are numerous Skye Princes in the various medbed channels, but the original is a short-haired, ruddy-cheeked Australian woman named Kerrie-Ann Thornton, who claims to be a former medbed nurse with insider information. Though Thornton says on her website that the medbeds will be free, she and her impersonators also serve as a gateway to the many ways you can currently spend money on medbeds. There’s a Swiss company, for example, that sells “virtual medbeds”—$2,500 for nothing but the vague promise of DNA reprogramming. A U.S.-based chain of medbed operators called Tesla BioHealing has been flagged by federal regulators for claiming that its “Life Force Energy” treatments can cure stroke paralysis, epilepsy, Alzheimer’s, and terminal cancer. Some operators simply ask for fees to jump customers ahead on “waiting lists” when the medbeds do eventually arrive. I never sent any money to the National Quantum Coordination Office, so I never got the answers the appointment promised. But despite the typos and inconsistencies in the emails, I understood how such an unsophisticated scam might actually be effective if the mark was sufficiently confused—or frightened. I thought of one 64-year-old woman named Johna, with whom I had been emailing. Johna told me she believed that the medbeds would cure her metastatic breast cancer. She no longer has any trust in established medicine, not after watching the deaths of two of her own children, which convinced her that doctors intentionally keep patients sick to “keep the money coming in.” So when she discovered a medbed Facebook page two years ago, she was intrigued. She began corresponding with an official-sounding Skye Prince account on Messenger and filled out an application for a healing appointment. Following instructions, Johna bought a Vanilla Visa gift card, loaded it with $300, and prepared to pay her medbed appointment fee. As Johna was about to send her information over, she suddenly grew suspicious. She decided to question the account, and it eventually admitted that it wasn’t the real Skye but someone doing administrative work on her behalf. Johna backed out of the chat, realizing that she’d only just escaped being scammed. But the near miss didn’t scare her away from medbeds. Instead, she decided she needed to know more about the real Kerrie-Ann Thornton. She sought out other Facebook groups. She was impressed that the real Skye Prince was calling out the copycat accounts and that she insisted the beds would be free. “I believed her words to be genuine,” she wrote to me. There was, as I’d discover, a lot deeper to go. It is impossible to separate the rise of medbeds from the mutant growth of QAnon. QAnon, as many people think of it, is long gone; Donald Trump never fulfilled QAnon’s original promises, and the figure known as “Q” went silent years ago. But in the collapse, Q’s followers dispersed and attached themselves to new communities and interests, and today QAnon’s form is a vast tangle of fantastical and often contradictory storylines. The medbeds conspiracy theory is part of this mess. The QAnon DNA is right there: Somewhere secret, perhaps in deep underground bases, there are medbeds. Some nefarious force is keeping them from the public—or, alternatively, medbeds are being kept a secret by the Good Guys for nobler and more strategic reasons. Soon enough, though, Trump will reveal the beds, and a new age of vitality will begin. Sometimes, the medbeds promise patriots more than just healing: near immortality, or something even more dazzling. One QAnon account I found argued that medbeds would activate our 10 strands of dormant DNA, giving us telepathic abilities. One woman, whom we’ll call Anna, told me of an aunt who, like many medbed believers, carries on all the old QAnon ideas: a still-living John F. Kennedy, secret reptilians in the British royal family, Democrats who eat babies. Anna is staying with her aunt in hopes of persuading her to visit a doctor for severe neurological pain. Anna has instead been able to see how bad it’s gotten: Her aunt is so certain of the medbeds’ imminent arrival that she won’t seek medical help for a broken arm. Her aunt has converted $600 into worthless Iraqi dinars and Zimbabwean dollars that she thinks she’ll be able to use to get access to the technology when it becomes available. You can find similar Q-colored chatter of medbeds on X, Facebook, Rumble, Gab, Bitchute, Parler, and elsewhere. These kinds of discussions have been common on these platforms since 2022. But go back earlier, and the talk starts to dwindle. Before 2018, it disappears: The platforms are almost entirely void of the medbed conspiracy. Instead, when people spoke about medbeds, it was in the context of science fiction. Star Trek had do-it-all hospital beds called biobeds. The 2012 film Prometheus featured doctor-free MedPods. Most on the nose was 2013’s Elysium, in which healthcare is sporadic on a post-collapse Earth. On the Elysium orbital space station, Med-Bays (some of them Versace-branded) cure the superrich’s every ailment. I had for some time wondered whether Elysium had been the inspiration, but my suspicions were heightened when, upon watching, I discovered that the movie’s premise is a Matt Damon–led quest for healthcare access that ends with the Med-Bays auto-deploying to the masses. I even called Phil Ivey, Elysium’s production designer, at his home in New Zealand. He told me that a scam artist had once emailed him about a plan to sell sessions in a “wellness/Anti-aging device as introduced in the blockbuster movie ‘Elysium’!” Ivey had designed Med-Bays to look like real-world MRIs, after all. There have always been overeager people who, through either extreme wishful thinking or some quirk of brain chemistry, have come to believe that science fiction hides secret messages or covers up for real events. But for a long time, these had been isolated individuals, too few and sparse to have any kind of cultural imprint, and certainly lacking the clout to land a video in the social media feed of the president of the United States. But somewhere along the way, that changed. Understanding exactly how that happened and when is critical, and not just for my quest to get to medbeds’ patient zero. It helps us to understand the broader information nightmare in which we’re now living. There’s nothing new about vaccine trutherism, or a belief in a global cabal of elites, or faith in an all-curing snake oil. What’s new is that those theories have gained enough power to alter our daily lives: a Health and Human Services secretary undermining vaccine access for children; a president whose pardon policy is premised on the myth that the deep state stole the 2020 election; the world’s richest man insisting that elites are carting in immigrants to destroy the political power of white people. This is not the first age with conspiracy theories. We’re just living through a time when they’ve escaped containment. I thought discovering how medbeds had made the jump from science fiction to QAnon would help me understand. I needed to know if there was some cynical political operative or gleeful nihilist behind this mess. I wanted to find out if the conspiracy theory’s originator had known what they were doing, if they now felt delight at the heights their creation had reached. I became obsessed. So I threw myself down one rabbit hole after another. I spent weeks at a time poring over old social media posts. I worked with researchers who’d had the foresight to maintain archives of 4chan’s main politics channel. I listened to obscure podcasts and read hyperniche blogs. I spent fruitless hours testing my employer’s patience, neglecting my more immediate work while dredging up zero-retweet Twitter posts from the early 2010s. But it was YouTube that finally cracked it for me. The first medbed mentions, it turned out, came not in Trump-loving channels or via QAnon posters. Instead, they appeared in a different online enclave entirely: the New Age movement, which takes a more metaphysical approach to reality. Spiritually inclined YouTubers discussed medbed healing in videos about frequencies, crystals, and fifth-dimension consciousness. This talk had proliferated in these corners of the internet as early as 2015—three years before MedBeds landed in QAnon spaces. In these forums, one name kept coming up: Captain Randy Cramer. In one video I found, Cramer, a serious-looking man in his 40s, discussed “holographic regenerating medical beds” back in 2015. In it, he referenced comments made in 2014, though they seem to no longer exist on the internet. This was and still is the earliest recorded mention I could find. I was fairly certain I had identified the conspiracy theory’s starting point. I left Cramer a voicemail. Had he been the first to talk about medbeds? Would he be willing to speak to me about them? Two days later, I received a call. Yes, he said, he had been the first. And he was happy to talk. I bought a ticket to Sedona, Arizona, to meet Cramer in person. I arrived in Arizona with a heaviness hanging over me. I had seen so much ugliness online. I had heard so many stories of real-world harm inflicted on sick and elderly people. And here I was, headed to meet the man I believed had started it all. I was apprehensive. But Sedona, one of the great New Age capitals of the world, almost immediately shifted my perspective. Pink Jeeps shuttled tourists to healing vortexes. Every third shop stocked bins of crystals. A woman I met during a UFO tour said she had signed up for a “healing chamber” experience from a vendor that also offered telekinetic spoon-bending lessons. At a store selling harmonic healing devices, I watched a man cradle a swaddled Chihuahua within a 9-foot-tall golden pyramid structure in an attempt to heal the dog’s vertigo. In this context, medbeds seemed conceptually like fun oddities, akin more to palm readings than to predatory disinformation. I decided I had to try out a medbed for myself. I found Sedona Quantum Consciousness online, via an advertisement for its Cell-Well Medbed, which appeared in photos to be a souped-up tanning bed. This wasn’t the kind of medbed featured in QAnon chats and scam pages: These real-life “medbed” experiences provide significantly less spectacular promises and don’t claim to be using classified government information. Still, there were similarities. The bed I would try was actually a “tachyon chamber,” a device that, the owner assured me over the phone, was even more like a real medbed than the one I’d seen online. I was told it had healed diabetes sores, infertility, a frozen shoulder, and the common cold. It cost $75 for a 20-minute session. I met the owner, Ada Tourtsakis, at her yurt on the outer edge of Sedona’s civilization. I was ushered in and asked not to take any photos of the tachyon chamber, which occupied its own inner sanctum in the yurt and was the proprietary technology of a man known only as Cobra. The tachyon chamber turned out to be, essentially, a narrow room. On the floor was a single mattress, low to the ground, with carnation-pink satin sheets. Quartz crystals sat at each of the bed’s four corners and atop a small glass table to the side. A pyramid of gold-colored rods was situated over the bed. Tourtsakis had me lie down and shuffle upward until the pyramid’s center was over my hips. She turned off the light and closed the door, leaving me with instructions to be open-minded. For 20 minutes I lay alone, in the dark, mulling over what I’d been told. Tourtsakis explained it to me like this: The process relied on the crystals in the room, which were quantum entangled with particles out in space. Once activated, they created a kind of wormhole in the pyramid through which altruistic aliens from the star cluster Pleiades would emerge. The Pleiadians would then harness spiritual energy from subatomic tachyon particles in order to heal me, performing what Tourtsakis referred to as “surgeries.” Pleiadians, common figures in New Age cosmology, are largely spiritual entities that guide humans toward higher levels of consciousness. In medbed lore, they are one of several kinds of aliens cited to explain the super-advanced “dimensional” medical technology. Tourtsakis had told me that the Pleiadians were the ones who had decided that the pyramid’s center should be at my hips, identifying them as the source of my chronic knee pain. If I wanted, she said, they could also direct me to a new career or my soulmate. I asked if I needed to believe in them for these things to work. She said it didn’t matter. They would help anyway. The New Age movement isn’t entirely apolitical. Many New Agers do believe in a coming Trump deliverance, and there has therefore always been a New Age contingent within QAnon. Tourtsakis herself believes that Trump will deliver the real medbeds. But in practice, Tourtsakis’ operation felt very different from the version of medbeds I had found on the internet. She told me that most of her clients were tourists seeking spiritual experiences, not desperate sick people. I knew she wasn’t promising a cure for cancer. And though my knee pain hadn’t been fixed, I found the experience relaxing. This felt recreational—not dangerous. Still, I was aware how dark this could all get. As I walked out into the desert sunshine, I tried to reconcile the suffering I’d seen online with aliens performing metaphysical microsurgeries throughout my body. I was stuck, but only temporarily. Because the next day, I was going to meet the man who had started it all. Here’s what Randy Cramer says happened to Randy Cramer. In 1969, a secret branch of the U.S. Marines implanted an unwitting woman with a genetically modified embryo in order to produce one of its new class of supersoldiers. That secret branch then swooped in to train the resulting child—Cramer—starting around age 3 or 4. Cramer’s family was unaware of this middle-of-the-night training thanks to “trace amounts of time travel” that would send him right back to bed as if nothing had happened. At age 17, Cramer was sent off to fulfill his 30-year service in the Marines’ secret space program. Much of that time was spent in the Mars Defense Force, which protected Earth colonies on Mars. “It was pretty much just fight bugs, fight reptiles,” he said. Then, abruptly, he was returned to his childhood bedroom, a healthy 17-year-old again. This had been accomplished with time travel and cloning technology. And though he has now recovered the full story, at the time, his memory had been wiped. He was left with a vague feeling of waking from a very long dream. He moved through his late teen years with a nagging sense he was different from others. At around age 19 or 20, he began having regular night terrors and daily flashbacks to “horrific, nightmarish things.” This went on for almost a decade, until, with the guidance of a therapist, he diagnosed himself with post-traumatic stress disorder. He began a yearslong meditation regimen to find the source of that trauma. By his mid-30s, he had unlocked years of memories from his time fighting battles in space. One of the earliest memories he discovered was of his first medbed experience. It had come in the heat of battle on Mars, after a reptilian alien ripped his arm off. Medics whisked him to safety, gave him a pain blocker, and placed him in a bed with a recessed, plush mat that molded to his body. A mechanical arm emerged from the top, ending in what he described as a giant lamp head. It projected a 3D model of an arm onto the wound. The projection, through its extremely high resolution and particular frequency resonance, tricked his cells into new growth. After five hours of a painless electric tickling sensation, he had a fresh arm, and he was sent back out into battle. Cramer told me all this, with remarkable directness, in a lengthy interview in the basement where he records interviews for various UFO shows. Instead of his house, we were at a New Age event venue in a strip mall that also contains an apothecary, a dry cleaner, and a head shop. I had arrived to find Cramer waiting for me outside, smoking a joint, patient and pleasant. He turned out to be an ordinary-looking 56-year-old man with a buzz cut and salt-and-pepper beard. And he was, I was finally sure, the first to talk about medbeds online. Everything he said tracked with my understanding of the idea’s earliest use. He himself seemed certain no one had mentioned medbeds before him. No one seems to know who coined the term, but the label wasn’t what mattered; it was the idea that the military was hiding a device that could fix everything wrong with you. After discovering his suppressed memories, Cramer began teaching meditation classes for others seeking to unlock the mysteries of the mind. At first, he kept his extraterrestrial experiences to himself, he said, because he was afraid of the potential blowback. But one day, a person he calls “the brigadier,” who communicates through a device implanted in Cramer’s brain, reached out to tell him that the space Marines would support him going public. The military’s reasoning was that leaking some of the information about these interplanetary conflicts might reduce the shock to the public the day the real revelations came. And so Cramer became a whistleblower. In 2014, under the pseudonym “Captain K,” Cramer sat for a multihour interview for one of the bigger “exopolitics” YouTube shows. Suddenly, he was a known name in the secret-space-program community, as one of the initial such firsthand experiencers to come forward. He began giving presentations at UFO events and interviews on paranormal programs. From there, medbeds filtered out to the rest of the secret-space-program community and to other alternative subcultures, including the New Age world. Obviously, Cramer’s claims, which hang on cloning and time travel, are out there. And it would be easy to conclude that he’s running a kind of big con. Cramer has earned money from his secret-space-program tale, selling lessons on “how to make brains smarter, faster, more effective.” More troubling, in 2017 he started a GoFundMe to raise money for the first medbed prototype. But he doesn’t seem to be riding off medbeds now, in spite of how popular they’ve become. The GoFundMe made only $8,700, according to the archived page. He had decided it wouldn’t be feasible to make a medbed, and he moved on. The problem is that the world did not, which was partially why he’d agreed to talk to me. He wanted to distance himself from the scams that he was seeing—and from the way he had watched this one minor detail in his life story snowball. Cramer has been going to conferences related to extraterrestrial matters for years, and he knows that his audiences have always been “outside the mainstream.” But starting around 2015 or 2016, the people coming to his talks had gone, in his words, “nuts.” This was the period when Pizzagate, the proto-QAnon conspiracy theory, began to scramble our political realities. “It starts attracting everybody,” he said of the forums he participated in and the speaking events he attended. “You start getting all the anti-vaxxers in, the New Age people, a bunch of—I don’t know what to call them, other than quasi-deconstructionist neo-Christians. I mean, to them, Jesus was an alien.” He held particular scorn for the flat-earthers, who he thought were plants to discredit genuine research about extraterrestrial technology. “I’ve done my share of saying, ‘That’s bonkers,’ ” he said, “and it makes people go, ‘You’re a member of the deep state.’ ” I asked him about the A.I. video that Trump posted of medbeds. Cramer’s no fan of Trump regardless, but the president doesn’t know a thing about medbeds, he said. It was simply him “pandering” to an audience. (Cramer, though also no fan of Democrats, thinks Trump is a “terrible person.”) Cramer is all too familiar with the medbed fallout. He watched, helpless, as MAGA and QAnon fed his healing technology into the exploitative machinery of politics. In an earlier era, perhaps, Cramer’s leaks about Martian bases and wormhole gates would have remained within his insular world of UFO blogs. But instead, it spread wildly, devastatingly out of control. I asked Cramer if he regretted telling the world about medbeds. He didn’t hesitate. “Totally, yeah,” he said. “It would have been way better off never mentioning that was a thing, knowing where we are now. I would have shut my mouth and never given people rope to hang people with.” He has spent years living with the consequences. “People started dropping their really heavy medical conditions at my feet, going, ‘Oh my God, please, somebody, can you help me?’ And I would just be like, Oh, I so wish I could,” he said. “That really shifted how I felt.” A dozen or so times a month, he said, he still receives emails from people asking where they can get treatments. “Their emails start out, ‘You’re my last hope of not being a crippled, miserable, dead person,’ ” he said. “It’s just heartbreaking emails. And I have to tell them, ‘I’m really sorry. It’s going to be a waste of time. I’m afraid I don’t have the tech.’ ” I walked away from our conversation with the conviction that the man who started medbeds was neither trying to make money nor influence politics. He was, instead, a true believer. Specifically, Cramer believes that humanity is on the cusp of a new, better era. Cramer’s vision doesn’t require anyone to send him money or rely on Donald Trump. Instead, he thinks humanity can deliver its own salvation—albeit with a bit of inspiration from aliens. In his telling, our species will undergo rapid transformation once we understand what’s possible. We will stop obsessing over money and fighting over resources and instead organize our society around generosity and kindness. When we’ve done that, we’ll join the rest of the universe in exploring the stars. He thinks this next step is inevitable. And in a just world, that’s where this would have started and ended: Cramer living in the boundaries of his fun, space-loving subculture, meditating in the Martian desert landscapes of Sedona, not hurting anyone. But that’s not the world we live in. In the world we do live in, a guy named Randy in Arizona, who just wanted to talk about aliens’ love of Earth beer and the annoyances of space-military bureaucracy, instead ended up creating a conspiracy theory that has baby boomers on Facebook refusing to go to the hospital and begging Trump to heal their broken bodies. I left Cramer that day feeling both deeply fascinated by his story and slightly depressed. I’ve worked at Slate for almost an entire decade, writing frequently about fringe conspiracy theories from dark corners of the internet. And over those years, those dark corners of the internet have expanded to be just, well, the internet. The conspiracy theories are no longer fringe: The president of the United States posted a video about magic, cancer-curing beds, and it was routine enough that it’s entirely possible I’m the last person still regularly thinking about it. What Cramer’s story shows is just how ruthlessly efficient the system behind that change is. The internet, suffused with popular anger, drawing from a polarized society, and built with rapacious algorithms, can chew up the most joyful of ideas. Cramer, I felt, didn’t deserve this. It didn’t seem right that he should have to carry the weight of what more-cynical people did with his idea. But that night, I continued to reflect on the answer I’d gotten to my medbed mystery. The more I thought about it, the more I realized there was something comforting about my conversation with Randy. Given all the suffering I’d seen, I had expected my medbed search to end with a villain. But there had been no evil mastermind. Instead, the problem was the information ecosystem of our era. As Cramer’s vision moved from one subculture to the next, various algorithms—and the incentive structures of social media—ensured that the wacky and wondrous aspects would be cast off, remaking the idea into a tool for manipulation. And yet somehow, strangely, I see something hopeful in that. If each conspiracy theory evolved from a dark mind, if human cruelty were the driving force behind all of this, there would be little hope for us. But if the systems around us are what’s responsible for the chaos, those can change. Not quickly, and not easily, but—and maybe I sound like Cramer here—societies can evolve. The day after talking to Cramer, I returned to the New Age center for a final event before heading out. I joined six others on mats on the venue’s galactically swirled green-blue floors. Cramer was also in attendance, sitting off to the side. A woman sprayed each of us with rose essential oil to help with grounding. She said she had co-created it with a higher-frequency being. Our host, sitting alongside his “BioCharger” machine, prepared us for a session in which he would rid us of parasites inside the body and also parasites out in the world. (We might have an additional bowel movement the next day, he warned.) Inside the machine, which looked like a large blender with a metal doughnut inside, blue and orange lights vibrated within glass tubes, while feathery electricity danced out the top. It cycled through a range of staticky notes—the frequencies that would heal us. After the session, Cramer, who was a regular at these events, would get down on the floor to show me all the different chambers within the host’s device, eager to explain its settings and how it worked. This, Cramer would tell me, was true quantum science, not something that operated just “off vibes.” But before we got to that, during the healing itself, I kept looking over at Cramer. It wasn’t just the kind of hard science he liked to geek out about that was going to make the difference here—our guide had told us to focus on love and forgiveness as we were healed. That, he had said, was part of the process. Cramer, I knew, believed in the healing power of meditation. He had said it was how he had healed his PTSD. I drove out of Sedona with the picture of Cramer still in my mind, sitting on a pouf at the edge of the floor, rigidly upright, eyes closed, hands pointing down and steepled together, meditating on forgiveness and love. Trump Went So Far This Time That Even Fox News Won’t Stand for It
2026-09-21

Trump Went So Far This Time That Even Fox News Won’t Stand for It

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-21

Visit Original Website
This story is adapted from the Slatest newsletter. You can sign up here to get it in your inbox every day. Liberals have long accused journalists of letting Donald Trump walk all over them, lying to reporters’ faces and using them as political punching bags even as he basks in the glow of their camera lights. But the press’s posture may now be changing. After Trump announced he was banning three news outlets from the White House, the major TV networks—including Fox News(!)—acted as one to protest the president’s decision. The dustup carries important implications for the First Amendment and journalists’ ability to tell the rest of us what’s going on inside this administration’s inner sanctum. With a Truth Social post, as Trumpian brouhahas often do. On Friday afternoon, the president put out a lie-filled, haphazardly capitalized post announcing he’d banned CNN, MS NOW, and Politico from the White House. Trump claimed the ban was “a result of their constant ‘reporting’ FAKE NEWS” and pledged that “Other Fake News Media Outlets” would get the same treatment. By Saturday, reporters from those three organizations were being denied White House access. CNN, MS NOW, and Politico jointly sued the administration, arguing that the ban “could not be a more direct assault on the First Amendment.” On Monday, the administration barred CNN from the “pool,” a rotating crop of TV journalists who film presidential events. So the other four major networks—ABC, CBS, Fox, and NBC—decided to suspend the pool indefinitely. Media outlets unaffected by the ban, like the Atlantic and the Washington Post, issued statements of solidarity. What’s lost when reporters aren’t allowed into the White House? In fairness, most reporters still are. Print, online, and radio journalists continued to follow Trump as normal today. But even though everyone has a camera in their pocket these days, the TV pool is how news outlets around the world get footage of presidential events in sensitive areas, like Trump’s Oval Office meetings with world leaders or appearances aboard Air Force One. Without it, the only footage the public will see from those places is what the White House wants it to. The administration has teased that it could soon replace poolers with its own video feeds, which would effectively amount to state television. “The public has a vital interest in receiving accurate, independent information about its government,” the five major networks said in a joint statement. “No administration should restrict a news organization because it objects to its reporting.” A federal judge named Timothy Kelly will hear the three banned outlets’ lawsuit. Trump appointed Kelly in his first term, but the judge sided with CNN in 2018 after the White House tried to yank a reporter’s press credentials. Kelly has scheduled a Wednesday hearing, which could make Trump’s ban short-lived. The president has preemptively vowed to appeal any adverse ruling. In the meantime, the ban is already having an effect. On Monday, Trump cut the ribbon to open his new White House helipad, but the TV pool was missing and the administration’s feed was inaudible, resulting in footage of the president speaking over the sound of static. Deciding which journalists deserve access to the White House isn’t an easy question. The press briefing room has a finite number of seats, and who should get them has been a contentious question under many administrations. But this is a naked effort to punish outlets for coverage the president doesn’t like—and it’s likely aimed at having a chilling effect. Being on site helps reporters cultivate sources and break news, which in turn helps inform those of us who don’t have access to the building. If covering the president aggressively risks getting your outlet banned, it pressures reporters to pull their punches or shy away from stories that could anger the administration. Trump has already used the bully pulpit and lawsuits to try to wring more sycophantic coverage out of the media. “There’s something wrong with a country that can allow people to write purposely negative stories,” he said in the Oval Office on Friday, shamelessly trampling the founders’ vision of a country in which a free press was sacrosanct. Trump’s ban is another attack on that freedom, and it’s unlikely to be the last. Looking for an evening escape from the latest erosion of our constitutional rights? My colleagues have some suggestions. Meet the men who still love Bitcoin: Cryptocurrency’s power has declined, but it still holds a powerful allure to some people disillusioned with America’s economic system. Nathaniel Moore went to Las Vegas to see why some lost boys are still pinning their hopes on Bitcoin. Read about a beloved author’s new novel: John Green, author of classic young-adult books like The Fault in Our Stars, hasn’t published a novel in a decade. Now, he’s back with his first-ever book for adults. Laura Miller writes that Hollywood, Ending is potent “enough to make you wonder why anyone ever thought his earlier books were just for kids.” Raise a glass to a classic cocktail: The margarita’s ingredients may be simple, but its origin story is far more complicated. N.C. Stevens digs into the drink’s history and tells a fascinating tale about Prohibition and the delicious outcome of cultural fusion between the U.S. and Mexico. Watch a scary new film: Video games are notoriously difficult to adapt to other media, but, according to Sam Adams, the new Resident Evil movie makes the leap from console to screen “with the right mixture of fidelity and irreverence.” And, if you’re a gamer but not a horror connoisseur, Nadira Goffe helpfully breaks down whether Resident Evil will be too scary for you, using Slate’s highly scientific Scaredy Scale. Whether you’re watching with excitement or hiding under the covers, I hope you have a great evening! See you tomorrow. Slate relies on advertising to support our journalism. News and Politics - Slate Magazine
Does James Talarico Have a Chance?
2026-09-21

Does James Talarico Have a Chance?

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-21

Visit Original Website
Also from Jim Newell, the Surge is your weekly political sanity check: What matters, what might, and what’s just political theater. Sign up here to get it in your inbox. In 2018 a captivating congressman from El Paso nearly turned Texas blue. In a race against Sen. Ted Cruz that captivated Democrats nationwide, Beto O’Rourke lost by a modest 2.6 points, the closest a Democrat has come to winning a Senate or governor’s race in Texas in modern memory. Although this performance was still a loss that earned Democrats exactly zero Senate seats, it augured a bright future for Democrats in the state. “Blue Texas,” foretold since the Obama era, seemed on track. That perception grew in 2020, when Joe Biden lost the state to Donald Trump by only 6 points in a presidential year, improving 3 points on Hillary Clinton’s loss in 2016. The state’s color was turning. And then, in 2024, Texas Democrats, as O’Rourke told me, “got what they deserved.” Kamala Harris lost the state by a whopping 14 points. While the country shifted rightward as a whole, the turn against Democrats among Latino voters turbocharged the collapse in Texas. Cruz easily won another term in the Senate. “Blue Texas” had never seemed further away. Which makes it all the stranger that only two years later, we’re doing this again. Because Democrat James Talarico really could win a Texas Senate race. The political-media industrial complex has been down this road before. No one—no reporter, operative, commentator, analyst, or person on the street—wants to look like an idiot by overhyping it again. I certainly don’t. But after weeks of speaking with politicians and experienced campaign hands in Texas, there’s no denying it: There’s more to this race than the usual hopium. “You’d have to work pretty hard to not recognize that this is different,” University of Texas professor James Henson, director of the Texas Politics Project, told me. “That this Senate race is different than any Senate race we’ve seen for a couple of generations, let alone any statewide race.” He noted that in the run-up to the 2018 Texas Senate race, O’Rourke never led in any polls. Meanwhile, Talarico has led or tied in all but one recent poll. “I think Talarico is just one of, if not the, best candidates we’ve ever seen,” O’Rourke himself told me. Democrats have not won a statewide race in Texas since 1994. Especially given their setback in 2024, it was clear going into 2026 that Democrats would need a perfect storm to win. They just might get it. The national environment is at Democrats’ backs. Talarico is a compelling and well-funded candidate—albeit one not without baggage—running against a scandal-plagued and poorly funded opponent in Attorney General Ken Paxton. The strong gains Trump made among Latinos in South Texas over the issues of cost of living and immigration are reversing over the issues of … cost of living and immigration. There’s a populist revolt over data centers and Flock cameras. Gas is expensive. Everyone is mad. But it’s not just the conditions. There’s something more fundamentally different this time in Democrats’ approach. What struck me as I was talking to Democrats about this cycle is that, unlike in previous runs that had their hopes up, they’re not relying on vibes to do their work for them. The past failures and overhyping has chastened them and offered much to learn from, and they may well be learning it: They’re not promising a new era of Democratic Party rule in Texas. They’re just focused on winning this race, this time, and not squandering the opportunity that has fallen into their laps. Democrats’ future—not just in terms of winning control of the Senate in 2026, but in terms of staying competitive with Republicans in the long run—depends on their learning to win in red states. Have they finally cracked the code? Why does the idea of Texas turning blue hold such fascination in Democrats’ hearts? Why don’t charismatic Democratic candidates from Mississippi or Tennessee make the cover of Vanity Fair, rake in record amounts of money, and capture Democrats’ national attention? It’s not just because Texas is the crown jewel of red America, the second-largest state in the country with the second-biggest allotment of electoral votes. It’s because, unlike many other comfortably red states, there was a period when its demographics were shifting in a way that aligned with those of the Democratic Party. During the Obama years, the state was becoming younger, more concentrated around metropolitan areas, more diverse, and more white-collar. This was the coalition that Democrats were betting on for transformative change across the country, the party’s then recipe for an enduring majority. The idea, then, was not that Texas Republicans were going to convert en masse to voting for Democrats. It was that their majority would be diluted away as Democrats registered more and more Latino voters, assured to be on their side. College-educated transplants would flood to the orbits of Austin, Houston, and Dallas. Suburban voters would veer away from the GOP. The appropriate way to interpret this information was, and is, that Democrats had the ability to make Texas a competitive state. The inappropriate way to handle it was, and is, to think that assumed demographic change guaranteed that Texas would inevitably turn blue and it was only a question of when. This latter stance can breed an idleness that, as we’ve now seen, ensures it may not happen at all. One of the first tests of this era of demographic promise was the 2014 governor’s race, when Greg Abbott ran for election against Democratic state Sen. Wendy Davis. Davis had gone viral in 2013 for a 13-hour filibuster against a state legislative bill restricting abortion. She had a compelling biography, as someone raised by a single mother who rose to graduate from Harvard Law School. Helping her along in the field was Battleground Texas, an organizing group founded in 2013 by former Obama aides with the goal of registering Democratic-leaning voters in the state. While Battleground tried to explain that it was playing a long game, its linkup with Davis, who was attracting extraordinary national attention, applied pressure on both to show immediate results. But it was an awful election year for Democrats nationally, and Abbott defeated Davis by 21 points. “That was partly our fault,” Davis told me in an interview of the way expectations got out of hand. “Because when you’re running a campaign, you do need to build belief that it’s going to be different this time.” She didn’t have as forgiving a stance for the group that helped her along: “The unfortunate thing is there were a lot of big promises and hopes made by Battleground in that cycle,” she added, “that, when they didn’t come to fruition, some of the folks leading that effort just moved on.” Perhaps given that hangover, the O’Rourke campaign started off with low expectations in the 2018 cycle. “The O’Rourke campaign was not at first built to win,” Jeff Rotkoff, a Texas Democratic strategist who directed campaigns for the Texas AFL-CIO in 2018, told me. “In fact, they were dismissive of efforts of others to help them win.” Indeed, they were dismissive of Rotkoff’s own efforts to help them: He described to me how O’Rourke initially missed out on the AFL-CIO’s endorsement because his campaign hadn’t asked for it. The whole operation had a general “too cool for school” attitude early in the campaign, Rotkoff said, in which it rejected opportunities to work with the rest of the party infrastructure. But then it grew into a proper movement—not just among liberals nationally, but in Texas as well. And yet, to some degree, O’Rourke’s campaign was still winging it. Texas Democrats I’ve spoken to over the years have a way of framing O’Rourke’s 2018 Senate run as not only a highly impressive achievement … but also something they’re comfortable Monday-morning quarterbacking. There’s a pervasive sense that maybe he could have won if he’d just done a few things differently—though what those things are is up for debate. Part of the problem, everyone seems to agree, is that the party infrastructure itself wasn’t prepared for a competitive statewide race. The gubernatorial candidate, Lupe Valdez, “was sort of a sacrificial lamb” in the governor’s race against Abbott, as Henson put it. And the primary focus of the national Democratic Party was on flipping several suburban House seats, not on driving O’Rourke across the finish line. Rotkoff wanted to be clear that these weren’t attacks on O’Rourke personally, and that “they learned a lot of lessons” in real time. The point of comparison, though, is with the Talarico campaign, where “the strategic political analysis was there from the start.” This time, as former Texas Democratic Party Executive Director Manny Garcia told me, Talarico “doesn’t bear the burden of having to do it by himself.” Democrats have recruited a candidate for every state and federal race on the ballot, no matter how doomed the contest, to ensure that persuadable voters everywhere are hearing the message. (Republicans have derided this move as a gimmick.) The state party is cooperating with the Texas Majority PAC—in other words, with George Soros’ dollars—to provide resources. And Democrats have landed a strong gubernatorial recruit in state Rep. Gina Hinojosa, who sits next to Talarico in the state Capitol, to both challenge Abbott in a difficult year—making him work unusually hard for his fourth term—and reinforce the message of corruption the two candidates are using against their opponents. Talarico is “bringing so much interest to Texas, lots of money to Texas, and it helps me and my appeal to certain constituents. So we are complementing each other in a way that is very helpful for the statewide ticket,” Hinojosa told me in an interview. “There is this synergy happening up and down the ballot that we have never had before in Texas, and I do think that is a difference-maker.” So Democrats have the candidate. They have the environment. They have the opponent. They have the ticket. They have the money. They even have the strategy. One is hard-pressed to find a more dramatic shift in voter behavior between 2016 and 2024 than that of Latino voters in Texas. Hillary Clinton, per exit polls, won this cohort 61 percent to 34 percent in 2016. Eight years later—and with Donald Trump remaining precisely the same person he was, saying the same things he’s always said, every day, over the course of that stretch—Trump won 55 percent of Latinos in the state. Results on county-by-county levels are jaw-dropping. As the Washington Post reported after the 2024 election, 13 of the top 15 counties in the country with the largest rightward swings between 2016 and 2024 were in Texas. They’re all majority Hispanic. Inflation and economic hardship, which led to fond remembrances of the Trump economy from 2017 to 2019. Chaos at the border. Working-class Latinos joining the broader working-class turn away from the Democratic Party. Voters being another generation removed from their newness to the country and attaining a more conservative socioeconomic status. Social conservatives responding to leftward shifts among Democrats on cultural issues. Name something, and the answer is probably yes. This helps explain Democrats’ losses, from Davis to O’Rourke to Harris: The party may have gotten the demographic shifts it was expecting, but it never translated into the political coalition it predicted. As James Henson put it to me, “The growth of the Latino population relative to the Anglo white population was going to be a lot more complicated than the demographical-destiny trope really took into account.” And just about every Democrat and Republican I spoke to for this story said that Democrats took these voters, particularly in the Rio Grande Valley, for granted—“based on the color of their skin or their country of origin,” as Beto O’Rourke put it. A better way to put it, though, is that Democrats never had these voters secured in their coalition the way they thought they did. “Latinos felt for a long time like Democrats were the lesser of two evils,” Sergio García-Rios, an assistant professor at UT and director of polling for Univision, told me, “but that Democrats never deliver.” Democrats “thought, for a long time, that the only thing that mattered was immigration, to Latinos, and immigration reform.” But immigration reform never materialized, anyway. And then, after the parade of inflationary and border-security maladies under Biden, it was time to give something else a shot. “There’s a lot of people who are working-class in these areas who voted Democrat for a long time, and their life hasn’t gotten any better,” Chuck Rocha, a veteran Democratic strategist—who is advising both Talarico and Hinojosa this year—told me. “And then along comes Donald Trump, who sounds a lot like, if you close your eyes, Bernie Sanders,” Rocha added. “He’s talking about a rigged system. He’s talking about remanufacturing jobs in America. That’s kind of how Donald Trump controlled that space while Democrats don’t spend any money juxtaposing that position, because there’s no real party infrastructure in McAllen or in Brownsville. “And that’s the perfect storm,” he concluded, “that put us in a position where not all of those people automatically came on board like their mothers and grandmothers.” In the long run, Democrats’ ability to win back Latino working-class voters will hinge on their ability to win back working-class voters more broadly. But as far as 2026 goes, the upside for Democrats with Latino voters’ swingy voting preferences is that they can swing right back from Republicans’ 2024 highs. And they can even do so largely on the same issues with which Trump won those voters: the cost of living, and immigration. Manny Garcia, formerly of the Texas Democratic Party, told me that “people wanted something in economic terms that was being promised” by Trump. He added: “And right now, what people are seeing is, ‘Well, my prices aren’t going down like you promised, I’m not having a better economic outlook like you promised, and I am getting consistently disrespected. My family’s getting consistently disrespected. My community’s getting consistently disrespected.’ ” On immigration, Brendan Steinhauser, a Republican strategist and former campaign manager for John Cornyn, noted that Latinos in South Texas “want a secure border, don’t want the cartels to run rampant, we don’t like human trafficking, we don’t want to see immigrants dying in the desert of thirst.” At the same time, though, the current Trump administration has gone well beyond what they find acceptable. Now they also realize they “don’t want these excesses, don’t want the federal government to come in and round up everyone, especially people that don’t have a criminal past.” As Steinhauser put it: “There’s not a lot of support among Hispanic Texans in South Texas for doing mass deportation of people who are not violent criminals.” The polling shows clear signs of a reversion, with polls of Latinos in Texas backing down hard from Trump’s 2024 margins while Talarico enjoys strong support among them in his race against Paxton. A recent survey from Univision and YouGov showed Talarico leading among Latinos 56 percent to 37 percent. This flip is worth understanding specifically. Because it’s not that all of the voters Trump picked up in 2024 suddenly, in the words of Rocha, are “going to run up and become democratic socialists or start dyeing their hair blue or get a nose ring.” It just means that some of those who felt that promises made to them were broken could sit this election out. “Not all of those guys are coming back, and they still think that Democrats are queers or weak,” he added. “But they’re really pissed at Republicans.” As much as this is Texas Democrats’ best opportunity in modern memory to win a prestigious statewide race, it is, ultimately, only that: an opportunity. And James Talarico is a long way from winning this race. Talarico has been ahead in polls over the summer, with strong support from independents, but he’s rarely polling above 50 percent. The great question is whether Texas Republicans will come home for their candidate, warts and all. Their candidate has behaved confusingly. Ken Paxton has been running an anti-campaign at times so baffling that it’s worth considering whether his ability to get others to do his work for him is actually genius. To quote his felled primary opponent, Sen. John Cornyn, “Paxton’s a poor campaigner” who’s “lazy and doesn’t raise money. And he basically is not offering a very compelling case for his election.” As of the latest filing deadline, which covers the first two quarters of the year, Paxton had only $16.7 million (with joint fundraising committee hauls factored in), while Talarico had raised $72 million. Paxton was literally on vacation in London, famously the home of the British monarchy, with his girlfriend on America’s 250th anniversary. When he does bother to campaign, he makes peculiar choices, such as at a late August event for which he wore a Four Seasons Orlando–branded golf shirt and what appeared to be a $40,000 watch. All this, of course, is layered atop his history of having been impeached by the state House, having faced an untold number of corruption allegations, and having been divorced by his wife on “biblical grounds.” That he’s made it this far is because he’d remained in Trump’s favor after trying to overturn the 2020 election through a lawsuit hastily dismissed by the Supreme Court. What Paxton must have known, though, is that once he became the nominee, he’d be golden. National Republicans can’t afford to lose his race—outside money would have to swarm to prop him up. And, sure enough, the cavalry has finally arrived. After a lengthy finger-pointing match between Trump, Senate Republicans, and Texas donors about who was responsible for rescuing Paxton, they’re all chipping in. Between the recent commitments from Trump’s super PAC, Senate Republican leaders’ super PAC, and others—including Elon Musk—Paxton managed to close the spending gap with Talarico in a week. That money will allow Republicans to amplify Talarico’s most prominent weakness: his history of getting carried away during what is now called “Woke 1.0.” In 2020 and 2021, Talarico said that “God is non-binary,” that there are six biological sexes, and that “white skin gives me and every white American immunity from the virus” of racism. More recently, CNN uncovered an appearance by Talarico in a 2020 Sierra Club forum in which he said that he was “thankful that young activists have really drawn the link between climate change and White supremacy and capitalism in our country,” describing them as “oppressive systems” in need of “dismantling.” Talarico has tried to dismiss old comments like these as “cringey.” But with the right ad budget behind them, they could be just cringey enough to send Republican-inclined voters, noses held firmly, to pull the lever for Paxton. Then again, it’s quite something to see nearly $100 million in super PAC money dumped into Texas, in September of an election year, to save a Republican Senate candidate. The GOP’s late attempted rescue of Ken Paxton coincided with the Republican midterm convention in Dallas in early September, and Paxton’s role in this showcase was largely to serve as the butt of a joke. He himself gave lackadaisical remarks on the convention’s first night—alongside everything else, Paxton is also an unenthusiastic public speaker—to an indifferent crowd. For the rest of the convention, he was subjected to Trump’s regular insults about how he dresses poorly and looks bad. Paxton, waiting for his super PAC money to arrive, just stood there and took it. If Texas Democrats can’t beat this guy, in this environment, could they ever have a chance in Texas? It’s a question for Democrats in states across the country this year. Each red state in which Democrats are competitive in 2026 is a little different. Ohio and Iowa are less diverse but have more recent Democratic DNA in them, having voted for Barack Obama twice. Right now they’re being hit hard by Trump’s trade (and real) wars. Alaska is uniquely reliant on the federal government and has a large independent streak. What Texas, Alaska, Iowa, and Ohio all have in common is the national wind at their backs, strong candidates, and decisions by Trump that went out of their way to make life in their states more difficult. They also all prompt a lot of heartburn about races over the past decade that fell short despite initial signs of promise and alluring polling numbers the September before the election. Should Democrats get swept in all these races, under these dreamlike conditions, Democrats nationally may, once again, forswear ever getting emotionally or financially invested in these states. (Or at least for another two years, when they find themselves inevitably tempted again.) The issue is that soon, converting red states where Democrats have lost their foothold—not just fantasizing about converting them—will become a necessity for the party. Republicans already have a structural advantage in the Senate with their strength in small, rural states, giving Democrats limited options and narrow paths toward a Senate majority. And after the 2030 census, Democrats are going to need some fresh electoral votes. States like California and New York are projected to lose multiple congressional districts, while various other blue states will likely lose individual seats. This gives Democrats fewer Electoral College options and will officially retire the old “Blue Wall” pathway to victory of winning Michigan, Pennsylvania, and Wisconsin (not that this pathway has been the most reliable recently). That means Democrats will need more options to win a presidential race. And there’s no better place to make it up—and beyond—in one fell swoop than Texas, to where many of those blue-state congressional districts will be transferred after the census. “We damn sure better be investing in Texas now than in 2032,” Wendy Davis told me. In other words, the long-term dream of “blue Texas” from the Obama years, seen as a way to lock in an inevitable, prolonged Democratic era, could soon become the medium-term need for “blue Texas” just for Democrats to keep up. The first order of business, though? Well, you’ve just got to win a race. Get the Surge in your inbox each Saturday. Slate relies on advertising to support our journalism. EU datacenter green scorecard finally escapes Brussels
2026-09-22

EU datacenter green scorecard finally escapes Brussels

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-22

Visit Original Website
Long-delayed sustainability labels land as Commission eyes minimum efficiency standards Brussels has adopted its long-awaited sustainability rating scheme for energy-guzzling datacenters, with minimum performance standards potentially next. The European Commission this week unveiled its Data Centre Energy Efficiency Package. It includes a common EU rating scheme and electronic label intended to make facilities' environmental performance easier to compare. A separate 12-week consultation asks whether minimum standards should be introduced to phase out the least efficient technologies. The scheme will cover individual datacenters with a power demand above 500 kW. Their electronic labels will draw on sustainability data operators already report under the EU's Energy Efficiency Directive, including energy and water use, waste heat reuse, clean energy, and interaction with the electricity grid. The first labels are expected to appear in 2027. If this all sounds familiar, that's because Brussels has been trying to get the scheme over the line for some time. The Commission's original plans for an A-to-G rating system were delayed after industry criticism. Moody's warned at the time that the eventual grades could have implications beyond embarrassing operators with a lousy score, potentially affecting financing and collateral requirements. A month later, reports emerged that Brussels was watering down parts of the proposal following lobbying from datacenter operators and tech giants. One contentious provision would have required clean-energy certificates used to offset emissions to come from the same region as the datacenter. The revised draft instead allowed operators to source them from renewable projects elsewhere in the EU. The package reflects a tension in EU policy. Brussels wants much more computing capacity for AI without a matching surge in pressure on electricity grids and water supplies. "Tripling our data centre capacity cannot mean tripling the pressure on our grids, our water and our energy bills," said Teresa Ribera, the Commission's Executive Vice-President for Clean, Just and Competitive Transition. "That starts with transparency, rating large data centre depending on their impact on our energy system. Efficient, flexible, powered by additional clean energy and reusing waste heat, this is how data centres become an asset for EU's energy transition." The labels may only be the start. A consultation on minimum performance standards for new and existing datacenters runs until December 14, with a legislative proposal planned for the second quarter of 2027. The Commission has yet to decide what the standards would require or which technologies they might rule out. Europe isn't alone in discovering that the AI infrastructure boom comes with some fairly substantial utility bills. California Governor Gavin Newsom this week signed seven bills tightening oversight of datacenters, including requirements around disclosure of water and electricity use and measures addressing who gets stuck with the cost of grid connections and other power infrastructure. The approaches differ, but the problem is much the same. Governments want more datacenters to feed AI, while electricity grids, water supplies, and the people paying for them are already feeling the strain. For Europe's operators, at least the first part of Brussels' answer has now arrived: show everyone how efficiently your bit barn runs. The question of what happens to the ones that don't make the grade comes next.  ® Did Copilot write the synchronization code? Register reader hit with surprise bill after Microsoft portals disagreed
2026-09-22

Register reader hit with surprise bill after Microsoft portals disagreed

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-22

Visit Original Website
Did Copilot write the synchronization code? A synchronization issue between Microsoft systems left at least one customer facing an unexpected bill after two portals showed different credit balances. A Register reader found themself on the hook for $16,000 in September after a balance that looked healthy had actually hit rock bottom and a subscription switched to pay-as-you-go. Our reader was one of many who signed up for Microsoft for Startups, a program that offers eligible businesses credits and other resources to build on Microsoft’s platforms. Amazon and Google have similar deals, but our reader signed up for Microsoft's version. According to our reader, Microsoft's Founders Hub showed a healthy balance of credits remaining as their company fired up services on Azure. They shared a screenshot from portal.startups.microsoft.com that showed plenty of credits remaining. Sadly, that was not the accurate figure. In reality, those credits had been exhausted in August, and the subscription switched to a pay-as-you-go plan, causing charges to mount quickly. Worse, the notification went to a former administrator who was no longer with the company, even though the account ownership had been transferred. Since August 17, the company has run up $16,000 in charges, mostly from AI workloads routed to Azure because the portal showed that credits were still available. However, a look at a different portal - in this case, the Azure Sponsorships portal - revealed the balance was indeed zero. Our reader shared an email from a Microsoft for Startups representative explaining, "The discrepancy you are seeing in the Microsoft for Startups portal is due to an ongoing synchronization issue between backend systems." The Azure Sponsorships portal was accurate. The Microsoft for Startups portal… not so much. "Our engineering team is aware of this behavior and is actively working on a resolution," wrote the representative. Very comforting when a surprise invoice arrives days or weeks before an automatic charge is made. Even more comforting was the comment, "We do not have an estimated timeline to share for when the synchronization will be fully corrected." And, of course, since the sponsorship credits had, in reality, been exhausted, "any additional usage may result in billable charges according to your subscription configuration." It is not clear whether Microsoft will waive the charges that were spotted too late thanks to its "ongoing synchronization issue." Our reader said they were told only the Startups team could grant an exception. The experience is a warning that just because one portal says a balance is healthy, another might tell an altogether different story. The challenge is finding that other portal and acting before a switchover happens and a credit card gets hammered in the name of AI progress. The Register contacted Microsoft about this tale of woe late last week, and the company asked that we give it until 1500 UK on September 21 to investigate. That deadline has passed without further comment. Perhaps our request is sitting in the wrong portal? ® Did Copilot write the synchronization code? Civo plots 40 edge datacenters to power Britain's sovereign AI
2026-09-22

Civo plots 40 edge datacenters to power Britain's sovereign AI

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-22

Visit Original Website
First site is due in Hertfordshire next March, with Nvidia's Vera Rubin systems and a longer-term 1 GW ambition UK cloud provider Civo plans to build 40 edge datacenters to bring sovereign AI services closer to customers, with the first due to open in Hertfordshire by March 2027. The longer-term target is 1 GW of capacity across the country, with Nvidia Vera Rubin NVL72 systems providing the computing power to run AI applications from UK-owned facilities. The Hertfordshire site is expected to open with 8 MW of capacity and eventually expand to 38 MW. Civo says it has secured five further sites in Lancashire, Greater Manchester, London, and Oxfordshire, which it expects to bring the total to 150 MW within three years. "For the past two years, we've surveyed 1,000 UK IT leaders about AI sovereignty," chief exec Mark Boost said at the firm's Civo Navigate event in London. "Last year, 61 percent said it was a strategic priority, and this year that's risen to 73 percent and that's really not a trend. That's the tipping point." Civo is pitching its UK ownership to customers wary of relying on US-controlled cloud providers. The US CLOUD Act adds to those worries because it allows American authorities to seek data from US companies even when it is stored in datacenters overseas. A recent report found that at least 80 percent of UK businesses using cloud services depend on AWS, Microsoft Azure, or Google Cloud. "Your data sits in a UK building, but the company is owned and controlled from overseas, so your data is still subject to foreign laws," Boost said. "Sovereignty isn't just where your data sits; it's about who has it and who they answer to. And in these uncertain geopolitical times, there's a growing concern around who holds the digital kill switch." The "kill switch" concern is not entirely theoretical. In June, a US export control directive prompted Anthropic to suspend access to two of its advanced models. The controls were lifted later that month, but the episode showed how abruptly access to a US-hosted AI service could change. Civo also argues that placing capacity closer to customers will reduce the delay between an AI request and its response. Nvidia estimates that inference – running a trained model – accounts for about 35 percent of AI workloads today and could reach 60 to 65 percent by 2030. Civo claims it will be among the first cloud providers in the UK to offer the Vera Rubin platform once its Hertfordshire site is operational. It is taking orders now, alongside existing capacity on Nvidia's older B300, B200, H100, and H200 instances. Early access pricing for Vera Rubin starts at $10 per hour. ® Did Copilot write the synchronization code? Campaigners tell Burnham not to trade away the UK's tech tax to please Trump
2026-09-22

Campaigners tell Burnham not to trade away the UK's tech tax to please Trump

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-22

Visit Original Website
Letter lands ahead of the PM's first face-to-face meeting with the US president Campaigners are urging Prime Minister Andy Burnham to defend the UK's Digital Services Tax (DST) when he meets Donald Trump in New York later today. Civil society organizations have written to Burnham and Chancellor John Healey, asking them to resist pressure to scrap the tax. Trump regards it as one of the European levies that create unfair trade barriers for US companies and has threatened the UK with a "big tariff" if it keeps the DST. Introduced in 2020, the DST applies a 2 percent tax to certain revenues from UK users of search engines, social media services, and online marketplaces. It applies to groups generating more than £500 million globally from those activities and more than £25 million from UK users, with the first £25 million of UK revenue exempt. The tax was introduced to address what the government described as a mismatch between where digital businesses generate value and where their profits are taxed. It is separate from corporation tax rather than a replacement for it. In their letter, signatories including Tax Justice UK, the National Education Union, 38 Degrees, Global Witness, and Cost of Living Action press the government to keep the DST and extend it to AI companies and other digital services. They also call for measures to prevent multinationals from shifting profits through low-tax jurisdictions. Faiza Shaheen, executive director at Tax Justice UK, said: "Under no circumstances should the UK be cutting taxes for some of the richest corporations to please Donald Trump. Let's not forget, these businesses are often huge monopolies that have enormous power and more money than entire countries. If the UK caves in to this demand, what will Trump and the USA come after next? Burnham should stand strong and strengthen the DST to include booming AI companies, with the revenues invested to ease the cost of living for people all across Britain." The tax raised £800 million in 2024-25. Its scope includes activities run by companies such as Amazon, Google, Meta, eBay and TikTok. With government borrowing rising, scrapping the DST would also mean giving up revenue ahead of Healey's first Budget. The campaigners argue that the government should collect more from digital giants, not less. ® Did Copilot write the synchronization code?
