DoorDash agrees to pay $131.5M to settle New York City probe into delivery worker pay
2026-09-22

DoorDash agrees to pay $131.5M to settle New York City probe into delivery worker pay

The Next Web | 2026-09-22

Visit Original Website
A DoorDash courier cycles through Chelsea, New York, in 2020. DoorDash has agreed to pay $131.5M to settle a New York City investigation that found it underpaid delivery workers or paid them late. The deal with the city’s Department of Consumer and Worker Protection (DCWP) ends a dispute that began early in Mayor Zohran Mamdani’s administration. “Simply put, we screwed up,” DoorDash said in a post about the settlement. “Our mistakes meant some Dashers were underpaid or paid late.” “While these mistakes weren’t intentional, that doesn’t make them okay. We pride ourselves on operational excellence at DoorDash – and this was not excellent, nor was it acceptable. Dashers should be paid in full, on time, every time. We are sorry to the Dashers we let down.” Most of the money is tied up in a disagreement over when payments should be made. More than $83 million will go toward figuring out how to pay couriers who are logged into the app and waiting for jobs between deliveries. Another $12.3 million covers payments that were missing or delayed, and $16.7 million will be paid to DCWP as fines. DoorDash attributed its errors to a combination of software bugs and unusual edge cases, citing examples such as deliveries that went beyond city boundaries, orders with multiple pickup or drop-off points, jobs that were canceled or only partially completed, and couriers who provided incomplete or incorrect bank details. The company states that it has sorted out the technical faults. New York’s minimum wage rules for app-based delivery workers started in 2023. DoorDash says these are the most complex pay rules for couriers in the country. The company also says its New York couriers have earned over $1 billion since then, with an average pay of about $30 per active hour before tips. The Mamdani government has treated the enforcement of those rules as a top priority. In January, the DCWP charged both DoorDash and Uber Eats with having caused couriers to lose more than $550 million in tips as a result of changes to their apps, and at the same time the city announced a separate $5 million settlement with three delivery apps regarding violations of the minimum pay rules, along with the reactivation of thousands of couriers who had been deactivated. Mamdani was scheduled to present the DoorDash agreement at City Hall on Tuesday morning, and City Hall called it a historic settlement for workers, according to ABC7 New York. DoorDash had already made a deal about workers’ wages in New York. In 2025, it agreed to pay about $16.75 million to settle claims by the state attorney general that it used customer tips to reduce couriers’ guaranteed pay. Andy Burnham wants the UK to use its G20 presidency to broker a global AI agreement

Andy Burnham in Downing Street on 20 July 2026, the day he took office as UK prime minister. Britain will try to broker a worldwide deal on AI during its upcoming turn at the head of the G20, Prime Minister Andy Burnham told reporters on the eve of the UN General Assembly in New York, Politico Europe reported. Britain was “uniquely positioned to play a leadership role on AI,” he said, pointing to its relationships with the EU, the US, and China. Then, he added that the country’s own expertise, including that of the AI Security Institute, could enable it to act as an “honest broker”. Politico described it as the most significant statement yet from Burnham on how his government intends to handle the risks of AI. Global AI governance has climbed up the diplomatic agenda after a series of stark warnings about where the technology is heading. Burnham stated that warnings from people knowledgeable about AI had made him think again, and added that he did not want to become what he termed a “doom-monger” about AI, committing instead to finding a middle ground between the benefits and dangers of the technology. The message which he intends to take to Donald Trump is that Britain plans to pursue a measured approach to AI. The meeting between the two is scheduled for Tuesday, marking their first meeting since Burnham became prime minister, and Burnham has said he will reassure the US president of Britain’s intention to take a measured approach to AI. As Burnham pointed out, Trump has acknowledged some of the risks associated with AI, even though the president has called warnings about the technology being existential a “HOAX”. Kanishka Narayan, who is the UK’s minister for artificial intelligence, is accompanying Burnham, and Downing Street has stated that artificial intelligence will play a dominant role in the prime minister’s diplomatic meetings in New York. Andy Burnham, the British Prime Minister, is due to give a speech to the General Assembly on Tuesday evening, in which he will ask countries to work together on the use of AI for good and has promised that the UK will take the lead on this issue. “I think we can really do something in this space [that is] very significant,” he told reporters. Anthropic and OpenAI ask Australia to ease its ban on AI training with local content
2026-09-22

Anthropic and OpenAI ask Australia to ease its ban on AI training with local content

The Next Web | 2026-09-22

Visit Original Website
Claude by Anthropic and OpenAI Logos on Smartphones, AI Rivalry Collaboration, Frontier Model Competition, ChatGPT vs Claude, Tech Giants Showdown Anthropic and OpenAI have asked Australian lawmakers to reconsider rules that stop them from training AI models on the country’s creative work, Reuters reported on Tuesday, citing the companies’ submissions to a parliamentary inquiry into AI laws. In October 2025, the Australian government stated that it would not include a text and data mining exception in its copyright legislation. In effect, this means that AI developers must obtain permission from copyright holders to train their machines on those works, a situation that in practice prevents mostly US-based laboratories from training models in the country. Both companies have acknowledged that decision in their submissions but are asking for some margin around it. Anthropic has acknowledged that broad copyright exceptions have been ruled out and has therefore suggested a ‘narrow form of conditional approval’ for the purpose of model training. Also, it stated that it would be open to conditions such as investment or other measures intended for the support of Australian creators and cultural work; OpenAI requested a balanced framework which would enable the models to learn from publicly available information while at the same time providing rights holders with opportunities to work together with AI companies. Both companies connected the question to their intended data center plans in Australia; OpenAI has a power purchase agreement with NextDC for a site it plans to build in Sydney, and Anthropic was announced last week as a partner in a data center project in Queensland. Anthropic has established that connection previously; when briefing notes were made public as a result of a request under the Freedom of Information Act, it was stated that Anthropic’s chief executive, Dario Amodei, had raised the issue of copyright with Treasurer Jim Chalmers in April, according to AFP. The officials noted that Anthropic would claim its investment in Australia depended on there being clarity regarding copyright settings. The creative industries, on the contrary, have made a strong effort. In July, Sarah Hanson-Young, a Green senator and chairwoman of a Senate inquiry into AI and data centers, stated that AI companies wishing to train on copyrighted material “should pay for it just as everyone else does”. Anthropic has also encountered this problem in court. In the United States, it agreed in 2025 to pay $1.5 billion to settle a class action brought by authors over books that had been pirated and used in its training data, following a ruling by a judge that training on lawfully acquired books could be considered fair use. The submissions were handed to a joint select committee on AI which both houses of parliament established in August. At the same time, Australia intends to introduce more comprehensive AI and data center regulations next year. Get the most important tech news in your inbox each week.
